Q:  In a video game, each enemy defeated gives you 8 points. If a level has 7 enemies total and you destroy all but 2 of them, how many points would you earn? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to calculate the total points earned in a video game level where each enemy defeated gives 8 points. The level has 7 enemies, but only 5 are defeated (since 2 are left). The strategy involves determining the number of enemies defeated and multiplying that by the points per enemy.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total enemies in the level: 7
- Enemies not defeated: 2
- Enemies defeated = Total enemies - Enemies not defeated = \( 7 - 2 = 5 \)
- Points per enemy defeated: 8 points
- Assumption: All defeated enemies give the same number of points.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1 (Direct Calculation):** Subtract the number of enemies not defeated from the total enemies to find the number defeated, then multiply by the points per enemy.
2. **Method 2 (Complementary Calculation):** Calculate the total possible points if all enemies were defeated, then subtract the points lost for the enemies not defeated.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### Method 1 (Direct Calculation):
1. Calculate the number of enemies defeated:  
   \( 7 - 2 = 5 \)
2. Multiply the number of enemies defeated by the points per enemy:  
   \( 5 \times 8 = 40 \)

#### Method 2 (Complementary Calculation):
1. Calculate the total possible points if all enemies were defeated:  
   \( 7 \times 8 = 56 \)
2. Subtract the points lost for the enemies not defeated:  
   \( 56 - (2 \times 8) = 56 - 16 = 40 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more direct and involves fewer steps. It directly focuses on the number of enemies defeated, which aligns with the problem's goal.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method:
1. Calculate the number of enemies defeated:  
   \( 7 - 2 = 5 \)
2. Multiply the number of enemies defeated by the points per enemy:  
   \( 5 \times 8 = 40 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result:
- Reverse-check: If 5 enemies are defeated, the points earned are \( 5 \times 8 = 40 \).  
- Cross-check with Method 2: Total points \( 56 \), minus points lost \( 16 \), equals \( 40 \). ✅

---

The answer is **40**.
EXTRACTED:
40.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
40.0

Q:  For Halloween Emily received 54 pieces of candy. She ate 33 pieces then placed the rest into piles with 7 in each pile. How many piles could she make? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Emily starts with 54 pieces of candy, eats 33, and then divides the remaining candy into piles of 7 pieces each. The problem requires determining how many complete piles of 7 she can make. This involves first subtracting the eaten candy from the total and then performing a division to find the number of piles.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total candy: 54 pieces
- Candy eaten: 33 pieces
- Candy per pile: 7 pieces
- Assumptions: All remaining candy is used to form piles, and only complete piles are counted (no partial piles).

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Subtract the eaten candy from the total to find the remaining candy.  
2. Divide the remaining candy by 7 and take the integer part of the result (floor division) to determine the number of complete piles.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the eaten candy from the total to find the remaining candy.  
2. Use repeated subtraction of 7 from the remaining candy to count the number of complete piles.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### Method 1:
1. Subtract the eaten candy:  
   \( 54 - 33 = 21 \) (remaining candy).  
2. Divide the remaining candy by 7:  
   \( 21 \div 7 = 3 \) (complete piles).

#### Method 2:
1. Subtract the eaten candy:  
   \( 54 - 33 = 21 \) (remaining candy).  
2. Repeatedly subtract 7 from 21:  
   - \( 21 - 7 = 14 \) (1 pile).  
   - \( 14 - 7 = 7 \) (2 piles).  
   - \( 7 - 7 = 0 \) (3 piles).  
   Total piles = 3.

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses division directly, avoiding the need for repeated subtraction. Method 2 is more time-consuming but can be useful for verifying results.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Subtract the eaten candy:  
   \( 54 - 33 = 21 \).  
2. Divide the remaining candy by 7:  
   \( 21 \div 7 = 3 \).  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
1. Verify subtraction: \( 54 - 33 = 21 \).  
2. Verify division: \( 21 \div 7 = 3 \).  
3. Verify by multiplication: \( 3 \times 7 = 21 \), which matches the remaining candy.

---

The answer is **3**.
EXTRACTED:
3.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
3.0

Q:  Adam earned 9 dollars for each lawn he mowed. If he had 12 lawns to mow, but forgot to mow 8 of them, how much money did he actually earn? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Adam earns $9 for each lawn he mows. He was supposed to mow 12 lawns but only mowed 4 (since he forgot to mow 8). To find how much money he actually earned, we need to calculate the total number of lawns he mowed and multiply that by the earnings per lawn.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Earnings per lawn: $9
- Total lawns assigned: 12
- Lawns not mowed: 8
- Lawns actually mowed: \( 12 - 8 = 4 \)
- Assumption: Adam earns money only for the lawns he mows.

---

### Step 2: Propose two solution methods
**Method 1:**  
Directly calculate the number of lawns mowed by subtracting the lawns not mowed from the total assigned. Then multiply the number of lawns mowed by the earnings per lawn.

**Method 2:**  
First calculate the total potential earnings if Adam had mowed all 12 lawns. Then subtract the earnings for the lawns he forgot to mow.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### Method 1:
1. Calculate the number of lawns mowed:  
   \( 12 - 8 = 4 \)
2. Multiply by the earnings per lawn:  
   \( 4 \times 9 = 36 \)

#### Method 2:
1. Calculate the total potential earnings for 12 lawns:  
   \( 12 \times 9 = 108 \)
2. Calculate the earnings lost for the 8 lawns not mowed:  
   \( 8 \times 9 = 72 \)
3. Subtract the lost earnings from the total potential earnings:  
   \( 108 - 72 = 36 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more direct and involves fewer steps, making it the preferred method.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
1. Calculate the number of lawns mowed:  
   \( 12 - 8 = 4 \)
2. Multiply by the earnings per lawn:  
   \( 4 \times 9 = 36 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Reverse-check: If Adam earned $36 and each lawn pays $9, then \( 36 \div 9 = 4 \), which matches the number of lawns mowed. ✅
- Cross-check with Method 2: \( 108 - 72 = 36 \). ✅

---

The answer is **36**.
EXTRACTED:
36.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
36.0

Q:  Katie had 11 songs on her mp3 player. If she deleted 7 old songs from it and then added 24 new songs, how many songs does she have on her mp3 player? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Katie starts with 11 songs on her mp3 player. She deletes 7 songs and then adds 24 new songs. To find the total number of songs she has now, we need to subtract the deleted songs from the initial count and then add the new songs. This involves sequential subtraction and addition.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial songs: 11
- Deleted songs: 7
- Added songs: 24
- Assumption: No other changes to the song count (e.g., no songs were lost or duplicated).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Sequential operations: First subtract the deleted songs from the initial count, then add the new songs.  
**Method 2:** Combine the operations into a single expression: Add the new songs to the initial count first, then subtract the deleted songs.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Sequential operations**
1. Subtract the deleted songs:  
   \( 11 - 7 = 4 \)  
   Katie has 4 songs left after deleting 7 songs.
2. Add the new songs:  
   \( 4 + 24 = 28 \)  
   Katie now has 28 songs.

#### **Method 2: Combined operations**
1. Combine the operations into a single expression:  
   \( 11 - 7 + 24 \)  
   Perform subtraction first:  
   \( 11 - 7 = 4 \)  
   Then add the new songs:  
   \( 4 + 24 = 28 \)  
   Katie has 28 songs.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more intuitive because it mirrors the sequence of events (deleting songs first, then adding new ones). **Method 2** is mathematically valid but less aligned with the real-world process described in the problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Subtract the deleted songs:  
   \( 11 - 7 = 4 \)  
2. Add the new songs:  
   \( 4 + 24 = 28 \)  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse-check: Start with the final count (28 songs). Subtract the new songs added (24):  
  \( 28 - 24 = 4 \)  
  Add back the deleted songs (7):  
  \( 4 + 7 = 11 \)  
  This matches the initial count, confirming the result.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **28**.
EXTRACTED:
28.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
28.0

Q:  For the school bake sale Carol made 30 cupcakes. If she sold 9 of them and then made 28 more, how many cupcakes would she have? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Carol starts with 30 cupcakes, sells 9, and then makes 28 more. To find the total number of cupcakes she has, we need to subtract the sold cupcakes from the initial amount and then add the newly made cupcakes. This involves subtraction followed by addition.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial cupcakes: 30
- Sold cupcakes: 9
- Newly made cupcakes: 28
- No cupcakes are lost or discarded beyond the 9 sold.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Sequential calculation: Subtract the sold cupcakes from the initial amount first, then add the newly made cupcakes.  
**Method 2:** Combine operations into a single equation: Add the newly made cupcakes to the initial amount first, then subtract the sold cupcakes.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation**
1. Start with the initial amount: 30 cupcakes.
2. Subtract the sold cupcakes:  
   \( 30 - 9 = 21 \) cupcakes remaining.
3. Add the newly made cupcakes:  
   \( 21 + 28 = 49 \).

Result from Method 1: 49 cupcakes.

---

#### **Method 2: Combined operations**
1. Combine the initial amount and the newly made cupcakes first:  
   \( 30 + 28 = 58 \).
2. Subtract the sold cupcakes:  
   \( 58 - 9 = 49 \).

Result from Method 2: 49 cupcakes.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** mirrors the chronological sequence of events (subtracting sold cupcakes first, then adding newly made ones). This makes it easier to follow the logic of the problem. **Method 2** is valid but less intuitive for this scenario.

Preferred method: **Method 1**.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Start with the initial amount: 30 cupcakes.
2. Subtract the sold cupcakes:  
   \( 30 - 9 = 21 \).
3. Add the newly made cupcakes:  
   \( 21 + 28 = 49 \).

Result: 49 cupcakes.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse-check: Start with the final total (49), subtract the newly made cupcakes (28), and verify the remaining amount matches the initial cupcakes minus the sold ones:  
  \( 49 - 28 = 21 \), and \( 21 + 9 = 30 \). ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **49**.
EXTRACTED:
49.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
49.0

Q:  For Halloween Debby and her sister combined the candy they received. Debby had 32 pieces of candy while her sister had 42. If they ate 35 pieces the first night, how many pieces do they have left? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding how many pieces of candy Debby and her sister have left after combining their candy and eating some. This involves summing their initial candy amounts, subtracting the number of pieces eaten, and ensuring the calculations are accurate.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Debby had 32 pieces of candy.
- Her sister had 42 pieces of candy.
- Together, they ate 35 pieces of candy.
- No candy was lost or added beyond what is stated.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
1. Add Debby’s and her sister’s candy to find the total.  
2. Subtract the number of pieces eaten from the total.  

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the number of pieces eaten (35) from Debby’s candy first, then subtract the remainder from her sister’s candy.  
2. Combine the remaining candy amounts.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total candy = Debby’s candy + Sister’s candy = 32 + 42 = 74.  
2. Remaining candy = Total candy - Candy eaten = 74 - 35 = 39.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Subtract candy eaten from Debby’s candy: 32 - 35 = -3 (Debby’s candy is fully eaten, and 3 pieces are taken from her sister’s candy).  
2. Subtract the remaining 3 pieces from her sister’s candy: 42 - 3 = 39.  
3. Remaining candy = 39.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
- **Method 1** is more straightforward and efficient because it directly calculates the total and subtracts the eaten candy.  
- **Method 2** is valid but involves unnecessary intermediate steps, making it less efficient.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Total candy = 32 + 42 = 74.  
2. Remaining candy = 74 - 35 = 39.

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
1. Add the remaining candy (39) to the eaten candy (35): 39 + 35 = 74.  
2. Confirm the total matches the initial combined candy (32 + 42 = 74). ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **39**.
EXTRACTED:
39.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
39.0

Q:  Haley's favorite band was holding a concert where tickets were 4 dollars each. Haley bought 3 tickets for herself and her friends and 5 extra tickets in case anyone else wanted to go. How much did she spend? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with calculating the total amount Haley spent on concert tickets. Each ticket costs $4, and Haley bought 3 tickets for herself and her friends, plus 5 extra tickets. The total cost can be determined by multiplying the number of tickets by the price per ticket. 

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Cost per ticket = $4
- Tickets bought for herself and friends = 3
- Extra tickets = 5
- Total tickets purchased = 3 + 5 = 8
- No discounts or additional fees are applied.

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct multiplication of total tickets by ticket price.
- Add the number of tickets (3 + 5 = 8).
- Multiply the total number of tickets by the price per ticket ($4).

#### Method 2: Separate calculations for each group of tickets, then sum the costs.
- Calculate the cost for the 3 tickets Haley bought for herself and her friends (3 × $4).
- Calculate the cost for the 5 extra tickets (5 × $4).
- Add the two results to find the total cost.

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:
#### Method 1:
1. Total tickets = 3 + 5 = 8.
2. Total cost = 8 × $4 = $32.

#### Method 2:
1. Cost for 3 tickets = 3 × $4 = $12.
2. Cost for 5 tickets = 5 × $4 = $20.
3. Total cost = $12 + $20 = $32.

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it combines the ticket count first and performs a single multiplication. 
- **Method 2** is valid but involves more intermediate steps, which are unnecessary for this problem.

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total tickets = 3 + 5 = 8.
2. Total cost = 8 × $4 = $32.

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse calculation: $32 ÷ $4 = 8 tickets, which matches the total tickets purchased (3 + 5 = 8).
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming accuracy.

### Final Answer:
The answer is **32**.
EXTRACTED:
32.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
32.0

Q:  Jerry had 60 pieces of clothing to wash. He put 40 of them in one load, but decided to split the rest into 5 equal loads. How many pieces of clothing could go in each of the small loads? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many pieces of clothing Jerry can place in each of the 5 smaller loads after he has already placed 40 pieces in one load. This requires calculating the remaining pieces of clothing and dividing them equally among the 5 smaller loads. The general strategy involves subtraction followed by division.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total pieces of clothing: 60
- Pieces in the first load: 40
- Remaining pieces to be split: \( 60 - 40 = 20 \)
- Number of smaller loads: 5
- Assumption: The remaining pieces are split equally among the 5 smaller loads.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Sequential subtraction and division
1. Subtract the number of pieces in the first load from the total: \( 60 - 40 \).
2. Divide the remaining pieces equally among the 5 smaller loads: \( \text{remaining pieces} \div 5 \).

#### Method 2: Direct division after subtraction
1. Combine subtraction and division into a single step: \( (60 - 40) \div 5 \).

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:
#### Method 1:
1. Subtract the pieces in the first load: \( 60 - 40 = 20 \).
2. Divide the remaining pieces among the 5 smaller loads: \( 20 \div 5 = 4 \).

#### Method 2:
1. Perform subtraction and division in one step: \( (60 - 40) \div 5 = 20 \div 5 = 4 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result: \( 4 \) pieces per smaller load.
- Method 2 is more efficient because it combines subtraction and division into a single step, reducing intermediate calculations.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
Using Method 2:
\[
(60 - 40) \div 5 = 20 \div 5 = 4
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify subtraction: \( 60 - 40 = 20 \).
- Verify division: \( 20 \div 5 = 4 \).
- Confirm that \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \), which matches the remaining pieces.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **4**.
EXTRACTED:
4.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
4.0

Q:  Robin was making baggies of cookies with 6 cookies in each bag. If she had 23 chocolate chip cookies and 25 oatmeal cookies, how many baggies could she make? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Robin is making baggies of cookies, each containing 6 cookies. She has 23 chocolate chip cookies and 25 oatmeal cookies, so we need to determine how many complete baggies she can make. This requires finding the total number of cookies, dividing by 6, and considering only whole baggies (ignoring any leftover cookies).

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Chocolate chip cookies: 23
- Oatmeal cookies: 25
- Total cookies = 23 + 25
- Each bag contains exactly 6 cookies.
- Leftover cookies cannot form a complete baggie.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
Add the total number of cookies first, then divide by 6 to find the number of complete baggies. Use integer division to ignore any remainder.

**Method 2:**  
Divide each type of cookie count by 6 separately, sum the results, and then check if combining leftovers could form additional baggies.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total cookies = 23 + 25 = 48.
2. Divide total cookies by 6:  
   \( 48 \div 6 = 8 \) complete baggies.

---

#### **Method 2:**
1. Divide chocolate chip cookies by 6:  
   \( 23 \div 6 = 3 \) complete baggies (remainder: \( 23 - 3 \times 6 = 5 \)).
2. Divide oatmeal cookies by 6:  
   \( 25 \div 6 = 4 \) complete baggies (remainder: \( 25 - 4 \times 6 = 1 \)).
3. Total complete baggies = \( 3 + 4 = 7 \).  
   Leftovers: \( 5 + 1 = 6 \).  
   Since 6 leftover cookies can form one additional baggie, total baggies = \( 7 + 1 = 8 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is simpler and avoids unnecessary calculations with remainders. It directly computes the total cookies and divides by 6, making it more efficient.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total cookies = \( 23 + 25 = 48 \).
2. Divide total cookies by 6:  
   \( 48 \div 6 = 8 \) complete baggies.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify total cookies: \( 23 + 25 = 48 \).
- Verify division: \( 48 \div 6 = 8 \) (no remainder).
- Confirm that 8 baggies use all 48 cookies.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **8**.
EXTRACTED:
8.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
8.0

Q:  A pet store had 19 siamese cats and 45 house cats. During a sale they sold 56 cats. How many cats do they have left? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding how many cats remain in the pet store after a sale. The total number of cats initially is the sum of siamese cats and house cats. From this total, we subtract the number of cats sold during the sale. This requires addition followed by subtraction.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Number of siamese cats: 19
- Number of house cats: 45
- Number of cats sold: 56
- Assumption: All sold cats are from the initial total, and no new cats were added.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Sequential calculation
1. Add the number of siamese cats and house cats to find the total number of cats initially.
2. Subtract the number of cats sold from this total.

#### Method 2: Combine operations
1. Subtract the number of cats sold directly from the sum of siamese and house cats in a single step.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:
#### Method 1:
1. Compute the total number of cats initially:  
   \( 19 + 45 = 64 \)
2. Subtract the number of cats sold:  
   \( 64 - 56 = 8 \)

#### Method 2:
1. Combine operations into a single step:  
   \( (19 + 45) - 56 = 64 - 56 = 8 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** breaks the problem into smaller, sequential steps, which may be easier to follow for some learners.  
- **Method 2** is more efficient, combining operations into a single step without intermediate results.  
Both methods yield the same result, but Method 2 is preferred for simplicity and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
Using Method 2:  
\( (19 + 45) - 56 = 64 - 56 = 8 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
1. Verify the total number of cats initially:  
   \( 19 + 45 = 64 \)
2. Verify the subtraction:  
   \( 64 - 56 = 8 \)
3. Reverse-check: Add the remaining cats to the sold cats:  
   \( 8 + 56 = 64 \), which matches the initial total.

---

### Final Answer:
The pet store has **8 cats** left.  
The answer is **8**.
EXTRACTED:
8.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
8.0

Q:  A new building needed 10 windows. The builder had already installed 6 of them. If it takes 5 hours to install each window, how long will it take him to install the rest? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine the total time required to install the remaining windows in a building. We know the total number of windows, the number already installed, and the time it takes to install each window. The strategy involves first calculating how many windows remain to be installed and then multiplying that number by the time per window.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total windows needed: 10
- Windows already installed: 6
- Time to install each window: 5 hours
- Assumption: The installation time per window is constant, and no interruptions occur.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Subtract the number of installed windows from the total to find the remaining windows.  
2. Multiply the remaining windows by the time per window to find the total time required.

**Method 2:**  
1. Multiply the total number of windows by the time per window to find the total time for all windows.  
2. Subtract the time already spent installing the completed windows to find the time required for the remaining windows.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1:**
1. Remaining windows = Total windows - Installed windows  
   \( 10 - 6 = 4 \)  
2. Total time = Remaining windows × Time per window  
   \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \) hours  

#### **Method 2:**
1. Total time for all windows = Total windows × Time per window  
   \( 10 \times 5 = 50 \) hours  
2. Time already spent = Installed windows × Time per window  
   \( 6 \times 5 = 30 \) hours  
3. Time for remaining windows = Total time for all windows - Time already spent  
   \( 50 - 30 = 20 \) hours  

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more direct and involves fewer steps, making it the preferred approach.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
1. Remaining windows = \( 10 - 6 = 4 \)  
2. Total time = \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \) hours  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- If 4 windows remain and each takes 5 hours, the total time is \( 4 \times 5 = 20 \) hours.  
- Verifying with Method 2: Total time for all windows is 50 hours, and 30 hours have already been spent. Remaining time is \( 50 - 30 = 20 \) hours. ✅  

---

The answer is **20 hours**.
EXTRACTED:
20.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
20.0

Q:  Vanessa had 13 music files and 30 video files on her flash drive. If she deleted 10 of the files, how many files were still on her flash drive? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many files remain on Vanessa's flash drive after she deletes 10 files. To solve this, we first calculate the total number of files she initially had, then subtract the number of files deleted. This involves basic addition and subtraction.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Vanessa initially had **13 music files** and **30 video files**.
- She deleted **10 files**.
- No files were added or otherwise altered during this process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the number of music and video files to find the total number of files initially.  
2. Subtract the number of deleted files from the total.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the deleted files from the total number of video files first.  
2. Add the remaining video files to the number of music files to find the total remaining files.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total initial files = 13 (music) + 30 (video) = **43 files**.  
2. Remaining files = 43 - 10 = **33 files**.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Remaining video files = 30 - 10 = **20 files**.  
2. Total remaining files = 20 (video) + 13 (music) = **33 files**.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one
- Both methods yield the same result (**33 files**) and are valid.  
- However, **Method 1** is more straightforward because it directly calculates the total number of files first and then subtracts the deleted files. This avoids intermediate steps and is easier to follow.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method
1. Total initial files = 13 + 30 = **43 files**.  
2. Remaining files = 43 - 10 = **33 files**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy
- Reverse-check: If 33 files remain and 10 were deleted, the total before deletion was 33 + 10 = **43 files**, which matches the initial total. ✅

---

The answer is **33**.
EXTRACTED:
33.0

Q:  Oliver had 35 dollars. For his birthday he got 50 more dollars but spent 84 on a new game. How much money does he have now? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Oliver starts with $35, receives $50 for his birthday, and spends $84 on a new game. To find how much money he has left, we need to calculate the total money he had initially (starting amount + birthday gift) and then subtract the amount spent. This involves addition followed by subtraction.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Starting money: $35
- Birthday gift: $50
- Amount spent: $84
- Assumption: No other transactions occurred.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
Add the starting money and the birthday gift first, then subtract the amount spent. This follows the sequence of events.

**Method 2:**  
Subtract the amount spent from the birthday gift first, then add the remaining money to the starting amount. This rearranges the operations but achieves the same result.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Add starting money and birthday gift:  
   \( 35 + 50 = 85 \)
2. Subtract the amount spent:  
   \( 85 - 84 = 1 \)

#### **Method 2:**
1. Subtract the amount spent from the birthday gift:  
   \( 50 - 84 = -34 \)  
   (This results in a negative value, meaning the gift alone was insufficient to cover the expense.)
2. Add the remaining money to the starting amount:  
   \( 35 + (-34) = 1 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more intuitive because it mirrors the sequence of events (adding the gift first, then subtracting the expense). **Method 2** introduces a negative intermediate value, which may be less straightforward for some learners.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Add starting money and birthday gift:  
   \( 35 + 50 = 85 \)
2. Subtract the amount spent:  
   \( 85 - 84 = 1 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse-check: Add the remaining money ($1) to the amount spent ($84) and verify it equals the total money after the gift ($85):  
  \( 1 + 84 = 85 \ ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **1**.

### Hypothesis:
Amy initially had 4 music files and 21 video files on her flash drive, totaling 25 files. She deleted 23 of these files. The problem asks us to determine how many files remain on her flash drive. The strategy involves subtracting the number of deleted files from the total number of files.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Music files: 4
- Video files: 21
- Total files: \( 4 + 21 = 25 \)
- Deleted files: 23
- Assumption: No files were added or recovered during the deletion process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct subtraction
Add the total number of files first, then subtract the number of deleted files:
\[
\text{Remaining files} = \text{Total files} - \text{Deleted files}
\]

#### Method 2: Subtract deleted files from each category
Subtract the deleted files from the total number of music and video files separately, then sum the remaining files:
\[
\text{Remaining music files} + \text{Remaining video files} = \text{Remaining files}
\]

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct subtraction
1. Compute the total number of files:
   \[
   4 + 21 = 25
   \]
2. Subtract the number of deleted files:
   \[
   25 - 23 = 2
   \]

#### Method 2: Subtract deleted files from each category
1. Subtract deleted files from the total number of music and video files:
   - Music files: \( 4 - 0 = 4 \) (assume no music files were deleted)
   - Video files: \( 21 - 23 = -2 \) (this is invalid because the number of deleted files exceeds the number of video files).
2. Since Method 2 leads to an inconsistency, it is not valid for this problem.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is preferred because it directly calculates the total number of files remaining after deletion without introducing unnecessary complexity or invalid assumptions about file categories.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Compute the total number of files:
   \[
   4 + 21 = 25
   \]
2. Subtract the number of deleted files:
   \[
   25 - 23 = 2
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify the subtraction:
  \[
  25 - 23 = 2
  \]
- Confirm that the remaining files are consistent with the problem's constraints.

The answer is **2**.

Q:  Chloe was playing a video game where she scores 9 points for each treasure she finds. If she found 6 treasures on the first level and 3 on the second, what would her score be? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with calculating Chloe's total score in a video game. She earns 9 points for each treasure she finds. To find her total score, we need to multiply the number of treasures found on each level by 9 and then sum the results. A general strategy involves breaking the problem into two parts: calculating the score for each level and then adding them together.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Chloe scores **9 points per treasure**.
- She found **6 treasures on the first level**.
- She found **3 treasures on the second level**.
- No other treasures or bonuses are mentioned.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1 (Level-by-level calculation):**  
   Multiply the number of treasures found on each level by 9, then add the results.  
   Formula: \( \text{Total Score} = (6 \times 9) + (3 \times 9) \).

2. **Method 2 (Combine treasures first):**  
   Add the total number of treasures found across both levels, then multiply by 9.  
   Formula: \( \text{Total Score} = (6 + 3) \times 9 \).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1:
1. Calculate the score for the first level:  
   \( 6 \times 9 = 54 \).
2. Calculate the score for the second level:  
   \( 3 \times 9 = 27 \).
3. Add the scores from both levels:  
   \( 54 + 27 = 81 \).

#### Method 2:
1. Add the total number of treasures:  
   \( 6 + 3 = 9 \).
2. Multiply the total treasures by 9:  
   \( 9 \times 9 = 81 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result (\( 81 \)).
- **Method 2** is more efficient because it reduces the number of intermediate calculations by combining the treasures first. This makes it simpler and less error-prone.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Add the total number of treasures:  
   \( 6 + 3 = 9 \).
2. Multiply the total treasures by 9:  
   \( 9 \times 9 = 81 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse-check: Divide the total score by 9 to confirm the total number of treasures:  
  \( 81 \div 9 = 9 \), which matches \( 6 + 3 \).
- Cross-check with Method 1: \( (6 \times 9) + (3 \times 9) = 54 + 27 = 81 \). ✅

---

The answer is **81**.
EXTRACTED:
81.0
GROUND_TRUTH:

Q:  Luke was organizing his baseball cards in a binder with 3 on each page. If he had 3 new cards and 9 old cards to put in the binder, how many pages would he use? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many pages Luke will need to organize his baseball cards in a binder, given that each page holds 3 cards. The total number of cards is the sum of new and old cards, and the number of pages required is the total number of cards divided by the capacity of each page, rounded up if necessary.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- New cards: 3
- Old cards: 9
- Total cards = New cards + Old cards = \( 3 + 9 = 12 \)
- Each page holds 3 cards.
- Pages must be whole numbers, so if there is a remainder when dividing, we round up.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:** Direct division with rounding up  
Divide the total number of cards by the number of cards per page. If there is a remainder, round up to the nearest whole number.

**Method 2:** Incremental grouping  
Group the cards into sets of 3 (the capacity of each page) and count how many groups are formed. If there are leftover cards, add one more page for them.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1: Direct division with rounding up**
1. Total cards = \( 12 \)
2. Cards per page = \( 3 \)
3. Divide: \( 12 \div 3 = 4 \) (no remainder).
4. Since there is no remainder, no rounding is needed. Luke will need exactly 4 pages.

#### **Method 2: Incremental grouping**
1. Start with 12 cards.
2. Group the cards into sets of 3:
   - First page: 3 cards → \( 12 - 3 = 9 \) cards left.
   - Second page: 3 cards → \( 9 - 3 = 6 \) cards left.
   - Third page: 3 cards → \( 6 - 3 = 3 \) cards left.
   - Fourth page: 3 cards → \( 3 - 3 = 0 \) cards left.
3. Total pages used = 4.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient because it uses direct division and rounding logic.
- **Method 2** is more labor-intensive but provides a clear visualization of the grouping process.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is preferred for simplicity.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total cards = \( 12 \)
2. Cards per page = \( 3 \)
3. Divide: \( 12 \div 3 = 4 \).
4. No remainder, so Luke needs exactly 4 pages.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Total cards = \( 12 \).
- Pages = \( 4 \).
- Cards per page = \( 3 \).
- Total cards on 4 pages = \( 4 \times 3 = 12 \), which matches the total number of cards. ✅

---

The answer is **4**.
EXTRACTED:
4.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
4.0

Q:  Gwen was organizing her book case making sure each of the shelves had exactly 9 books on it. If she had 3 shelves of mystery books and 5 shelves of picture books, how many books did she have total? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the total number of books Gwen has. She organizes her books such that each shelf contains exactly 9 books. She has 3 shelves of mystery books and 5 shelves of picture books. To solve this, we need to calculate the total number of books on the mystery shelves and the picture shelves separately, then sum them.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Number of books per shelf: 9
- Number of mystery shelves: 3
- Number of picture shelves: 5
- Total books = (books on mystery shelves) + (books on picture shelves)
- No shelves are partially filled, and no books are missing.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:** Compute the total books for each type of shelf separately, then add the results.  
Logic: Multiply the number of shelves by the number of books per shelf for each type, then sum the two totals.

**Method 2:** Combine the total number of shelves first, then multiply by the number of books per shelf.  
Logic: Add the number of mystery shelves and picture shelves to find the total number of shelves, then multiply by the number of books per shelf.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### Method 1:
1. Calculate books on mystery shelves:  
   \( 3 \times 9 = 27 \)
2. Calculate books on picture shelves:  
   \( 5 \times 9 = 45 \)
3. Add the two totals:  
   \( 27 + 45 = 72 \)

#### Method 2:
1. Combine the total number of shelves:  
   \( 3 + 5 = 8 \)
2. Multiply by the number of books per shelf:  
   \( 8 \times 9 = 72 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more efficient because it reduces the number of intermediate calculations. It combines the shelves first and performs a single multiplication, which is simpler and faster.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method
1. Total number of shelves:  
   \( 3 + 5 = 8 \)
2. Multiply by the number of books per shelf:  
   \( 8 \times 9 = 72 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy
- Verify using Method 1:  
  Mystery books: \( 3 \times 9 = 27 \)  
  Picture books: \( 5 \times 9 = 45 \)  
  Total: \( 27 + 45 = 72 \) ✅

- Reverse calculation:  
  Total books: \( 72 \)  
  Books per shelf: \( 9 \)  
  Total shelves: \( 72 \div 9 = 8 \)  
  Mystery shelves: \( 3 \), Picture shelves: \( 5 \), Total shelves: \( 3 + 5 = 8 \) ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The total number of books Gwen has is **72**.  
The answer is 72.

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with calculating the total cost for a group of 12 people eating at a restaurant where adult meals cost $3 and kids eat free. To solve this, we need to determine how many adults are in the group, calculate the cost for their meals, and ensure no cost is added for the kids. A simple multiplication of the number of adults by the cost per adult meal will suffice.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total people in the group: 12
- Number of kids: 7
- Number of adults: \( 12 - 7 = 5 \)
- Cost per adult meal: $3
- Kids eat free, so their cost is $0.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
Directly calculate the number of adults by subtracting the number of kids from the total group size. Then multiply the number of adults by the cost per adult meal.

**Method 2:**  
Calculate the total cost for the group by assigning $3 to each adult and $0 to each kid. Sum the costs for all individuals in the group.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

**Method 1:**  
1. Number of adults = \( 12 - 7 = 5 \).  
2. Total cost = \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \).  

**Method 2:**  
1. Assign $3 to each adult and $0 to each kid.  
   - Adults: \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \).  
   - Kids: \( 7 \times 0 = 0 \).  
2. Total cost = \( 15 + 0 = 15 \).  

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is simpler and more direct because it avoids unnecessary calculations for the kids' cost, which is always $0.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
Using **Method 1**:  
1. Number of adults = \( 12 - 7 = 5 \).  
2. Total cost = \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \).  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
1. Adults: \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \).  
2. Kids: \( 7 \times 0 = 0 \).  
3. Total cost = \( 15 + 0 = 15 \). ✅  

---

The answer is **15**.

Q:  A store had 50 oranges in a bin. If they threw away 40 of the old ones and put 24 new ones in the bin how many would be in the bin? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine the number of oranges remaining in a bin after some old oranges are thrown away and new ones are added. The strategy involves subtracting the number of old oranges discarded and then adding the number of new oranges added to the bin.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial number of oranges in the bin: 50
- Number of old oranges thrown away: 40
- Number of new oranges added: 24
- Assumption: No other changes (e.g., no additional oranges removed or added).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Sequential operations: First subtract the number of old oranges thrown away from the initial count, then add the new oranges to the result.

**Method 2:** Combine the operations into a single equation: Subtract the old oranges and add the new oranges simultaneously.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Sequential operations**
1. Start with the initial count: 50 oranges.
2. Subtract the old oranges thrown away: \( 50 - 40 = 10 \).
3. Add the new oranges: \( 10 + 24 = 34 \).

Result from Method 1: 34 oranges.

---

#### **Method 2: Combined operations**
1. Combine the subtraction and addition into a single equation: \( 50 - 40 + 24 \).
2. Perform the subtraction first: \( 50 - 40 = 10 \).
3. Add the new oranges: \( 10 + 24 = 34 \).

Result from Method 2: 34 oranges.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more efficient because it combines the operations into a single equation, reducing the number of steps. Method 1 is valid but slightly more cumbersome.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
Using Method 2:
\[
50 - 40 + 24 = 10 + 24 = 34
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Start with 50 oranges.
- Remove 40 old oranges: \( 50 - 40 = 10 \).
- Add 24 new oranges: \( 10 + 24 = 34 \).
The calculations are consistent and accurate.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **34**.

- Bags filled on Sunday = 5
- Total bags = 5 + 5 = 10
- Cans per bag = 4
- No bags were partially filled or lost.

**Method 1:** Calculate the total number of bags first, then multiply by the number of cans per bag.  
**Method 2:** Calculate the number of cans collected each day separately, then add the results.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total bags = 5 (Saturday) + 5 (Sunday) = 10 bags.
2. Total cans = 10 bags × 4 cans per bag = 40 cans.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Cans collected on Saturday = 5 bags × 4 cans per bag = 20 cans.
2. Cans collected on Sunday = 5 bags × 4 cans per bag = 20 cans.
3. Total cans = 20 (Saturday) + 20 (Sunday) = 40 cans.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:

1. Total bags = 5 + 5 = 10.
2. Total cans = 10 × 4 = 40.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial carrots: 48
- Discarded carrots: 11
- Newly picked carrots: 15
- No carrots are lost or added beyond these operations.

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:

1. Subtract the discarded carrots from the initial count.
2. Add the newly picked carrots to the remaining count.

**Method 2:** Combine operations:

2. Subtract the discarded carrots from the combined total.

   Remaining carrots: 37.

   \( 37 + 15 = 52 \)

   Combined total: 63.
2. Subtract discarded carrots:  
   \( 63 - 11 = 52 \)  
   Total carrots: 52.

Q:  Oliver was organizing his baseball cards in a binder with 3 on each page. If he had 2 new cards and 10 old cards to put in the binder, how many pages would he use? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many pages Oliver will need to organize his baseball cards in a binder, given that each page holds 3 cards. The total number of cards is the sum of new and old cards. The strategy involves dividing the total number of cards by the capacity of each page and rounding up to account for any leftover cards.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- New cards: 2
- Old cards: 10
- Total cards = New cards + Old cards = \( 2 + 10 = 12 \)
- Each page holds 3 cards.
- Pages must be whole numbers, so we round up if there are leftover cards.

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
**Method 1:** Direct division and rounding up.  
Divide the total number of cards by the number of cards per page and round up to the nearest whole number.

**Method 2:** Use modular arithmetic to determine if there are leftover cards.  
First, compute the quotient and remainder when dividing the total number of cards by the number of cards per page. If there is a remainder, add 1 to the quotient to account for the extra page.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### Method 1: Direct division and rounding up
1. Total cards = \( 12 \), cards per page = \( 3 \).
2. Compute \( 12 \div 3 = 4 \) (no remainder).
3. Since the result is a whole number, no rounding is needed.
4. Total pages required = \( 4 \).

#### Method 2: Modular arithmetic
1. Total cards = \( 12 \), cards per page = \( 3 \).
2. Compute the quotient: \( 12 \div 3 = 4 \) (integer division).
3. Compute the remainder: \( 12 \mod 3 = 0 \).
4. Since the remainder is \( 0 \), no extra page is needed.
5. Total pages required = \( 4 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
Both methods yield the same result. Method 1 is simpler and more direct, as it avoids explicitly calculating the remainder. However, Method 2 is useful for verifying whether rounding is necessary.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total cards = \( 12 \), cards per page = \( 3 \).
2. Compute \( 12 \div 3 = 4 \).
3. Total pages required = \( 4 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check
1. If each page holds 3 cards, then 4 pages can hold \( 4 \times 3 = 12 \) cards.
2. Total cards = \( 12 \), so all cards fit perfectly without leftover cards.
3. The calculation is consistent and accurate.

---

The answer is **4**.
EXTRACTED:
4.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
4.0

Q:  Debby's class is going on a field trip to the zoo. If each van can hold 4 people and there are 2 students and 6 adults going, how many vans will they need? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining the number of vans needed to transport a group of people to the zoo. Each van can hold 4 people. The total number of people includes 2 students and 6 adults. The strategy involves calculating the total number of people, dividing by the van capacity, and rounding up to the nearest whole number since partial vans cannot be used.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Number of students: 2
- Number of adults: 6
- Total number of people = students + adults = \( 2 + 6 = 8 \)
- Van capacity: 4 people per van
- Assumption: All vans must be fully loaded except the last one, which may be partially filled.

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
**Method 1:** Divide the total number of people by the van capacity and round up to the nearest whole number. This ensures that all people are accommodated.

**Method 2:** Use integer division to calculate the number of fully filled vans, then check if there are any remaining people. If there are, add one more van for the remainder.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### Method 1:
1. Total number of people = \( 8 \)
2. Van capacity = \( 4 \)
3. Divide total people by van capacity: \( 8 \div 4 = 2.0 \)
4. Since the result is already a whole number, no rounding is needed.
5. Number of vans required = \( 2 \).

#### Method 2:
1. Total number of people = \( 8 \)
2. Van capacity = \( 4 \)
3. Perform integer division to find fully filled vans: \( 8 \div 4 = 2 \) (no remainder).
4. Check for leftover people: \( 8 \mod 4 = 0 \) (no remainder).
5. Number of vans required = \( 2 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is simpler and more direct because it uses a single division and rounding step, while Method 2 involves additional calculations to check for remainders.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total number of people = \( 8 \)
2. Van capacity = \( 4 \)
3. Divide total people by van capacity: \( 8 \div 4 = 2.0 \)
4. Number of vans required = \( 2 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check
1. Each van holds 4 people.
2. With 2 vans, the total capacity is \( 2 \times 4 = 8 \), which matches the total number of people.
3. No one is left behind, and no extra vans are needed.

### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with calculating the total cost for a group of 15 people at a restaurant where adult meals cost $2 and kids eat free. To solve this, we need to determine how many adults are in the group, multiply the number of adult meals by $2, and ensure no cost is added for the kids. A direct calculation should suffice.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total people in the group: 15
- Number of kids: 9
- Number of adults: \( 15 - 9 = 6 \)
- Cost per adult meal: $2
- Kids eat free, so their cost is $0.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct calculation
- Subtract the number of kids from the total group size to find the number of adults.
- Multiply the number of adults by the cost per adult meal.

#### Method 2: Group-based calculation
- Calculate the total cost for all 15 people assuming everyone pays $2, then subtract the cost for the kids (since kids eat free).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct calculation
1. Number of adults = \( 15 - 9 = 6 \).
2. Total cost = \( 6 \times 2 = 12 \).

#### Method 2: Group-based calculation
1. Assume all 15 people pay $2 each: \( 15 \times 2 = 30 \).
2. Subtract the cost for the kids: \( 30 - (9 \times 2) = 30 - 18 = 12 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is simpler and avoids unnecessary intermediate steps. It directly calculates the cost for adults without assuming costs for kids and subtracting them later.
- **Method 2** is valid but introduces extra calculations that are not needed for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Number of adults = \( 15 - 9 = 6 \).
2. Total cost = \( 6 \times 2 = 12 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify: \( 6 \times 2 = 12 \).
- Confirm logic: Kids eat free, so only adults contribute to the cost. The calculation aligns with the problem's conditions.

---

### Final Answer:
The total cost for the group to eat is **$12**.

Q:  Sam was collecting cans for recycling. On Saturday he filled 4 bags up and on Sunday he filled 3 more bags. If each bag had 6 cans in it, how many cans did he pick up total? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the total number of cans Sam collected over two days. To do this, we need to calculate the total number of bags filled on both days, multiply that by the number of cans per bag, and sum the results. A straightforward multiplication and addition approach will suffice.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- On Saturday, Sam filled 4 bags.
- On Sunday, Sam filled 3 bags.
- Each bag contains 6 cans.
- No bags are partially filled, and all bags have the same number of cans.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total number of cans collected on Saturday by multiplying the number of bags by the number of cans per bag.  
2. Repeat the same calculation for Sunday.  
3. Add the results from both days to find the total.

**Method 2:**  
1. Add the total number of bags filled on both days first.  
2. Multiply the combined total number of bags by the number of cans per bag to find the total.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1:**
1. Cans collected on Saturday:  
   \( 4 \times 6 = 24 \) cans.  
2. Cans collected on Sunday:  
   \( 3 \times 6 = 18 \) cans.  
3. Total cans collected:  
   \( 24 + 18 = 42 \) cans.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Total number of bags filled:  
   \( 4 + 3 = 7 \) bags.  
2. Total cans collected:  
   \( 7 \times 6 = 42 \) cans.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 2** is more efficient because it reduces the number of intermediate calculations by combining the total number of bags first. This makes it the preferred method.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
1. Total number of bags:  
   \( 4 + 3 = 7 \) bags.  
2. Total cans collected:  
   \( 7 \times 6 = 42 \) cans.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Reverse-check Method 1:  
  \( 24 + 18 = 42 \) ✅  
- Reverse-check Method 2:  
  \( 7 \times 6 = 42 \) ✅  

---

The answer is **42**.

Q:  The schools debate team had 5 boys and 40 girls on it. If they were split into groups of 9 how many groups could they make? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks how many groups of 9 can be formed from a debate team consisting of 5 boys and 40 girls. To solve this, we need to calculate the total number of team members and divide that total by the group size (9). The result will be the number of groups that can be formed, and we will check if there is any remainder.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Number of boys = 5
- Number of girls = 40
- Total team members = boys + girls = 5 + 40 = 45
- Group size = 9
- Assumption: Groups must be evenly divided, and any remainder will indicate leftover members.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct division
Divide the total number of team members (45) by the group size (9) to determine how many full groups can be formed. Check for any remainder.

#### Method 2: Iterative subtraction
Subtract the group size (9) repeatedly from the total number of team members (45) until the remaining number is less than 9. Count the number of subtractions to determine the number of groups.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct division
1. Total team members = 45.
2. Divide 45 by 9:  
   \( 45 \div 9 = 5 \) (exact division, no remainder).
3. This means 5 full groups can be formed.

#### Method 2: Iterative subtraction
1. Start with 45 team members.
2. Subtract 9 repeatedly:
   - \( 45 - 9 = 36 \) (1 group formed).
   - \( 36 - 9 = 27 \) (2 groups formed).
   - \( 27 - 9 = 18 \) (3 groups formed).
   - \( 18 - 9 = 9 \) (4 groups formed).
   - \( 9 - 9 = 0 \) (5 groups formed).
3. Count the number of subtractions: 5 groups.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** (Direct division) is faster and more efficient, especially for larger numbers. It provides the result directly without requiring repeated calculations.
- **Method 2** (Iterative subtraction) is valid but slower and less practical for larger numbers.

Preferred method: **Method 1**.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total team members = 45.
2. Divide 45 by 9:  
   \( 45 \div 9 = 5 \) (exact division, no remainder).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Multiply the number of groups (5) by the group size (9):  
  \( 5 \times 9 = 45 \), which matches the total number of team members. ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **5**.

Q:  A toy store had 4 giant stuffed bears in stock when they got another shipment with 10 bears in it. The put the bears onto shelves with 7 on each shelf. How many shelves did they use? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many shelves are needed to store a total number of stuffed bears, given that each shelf holds 7 bears. First, we calculate the total number of bears, then divide this total by the number of bears per shelf to find the number of shelves required. If the division results in a remainder, we round up to ensure all bears are stored.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Initial stock of bears: 4
- Additional shipment: 10
- Bears per shelf: 7
- Assumption: All bears must be stored, and partial shelves are not allowed (round up if necessary).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the initial stock and the shipment to find the total number of bears.  
2. Divide the total number of bears by the number of bears per shelf.  
3. Round up if there is a remainder.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate how many shelves are needed for the initial stock and the shipment separately.  
2. Add the number of shelves required for each group of bears.  
3. Round up for each group if necessary.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation**
1. Total bears = Initial stock + Shipment = 4 + 10 = 14 bears.  
2. Divide total bears by bears per shelf: \( 14 \div 7 = 2 \).  
3. Since there is no remainder, no rounding is needed.  
   **Result:** 2 shelves.

#### **Method 2: Separate calculation**
1. Calculate shelves for the initial stock: \( 4 \div 7 = 0.57 \), round up to 1 shelf.  
2. Calculate shelves for the shipment: \( 10 \div 7 = 1.43 \), round up to 2 shelves.  
3. Total shelves = Shelves for initial stock + Shelves for shipment = 1 + 2 = 3 shelves.  
   **Result:** 3 shelves.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one
- **Method 1** is preferred because it directly calculates the total number of shelves needed without splitting the bears into separate groups. It avoids unnecessary rounding and is more efficient.  
- **Method 2** introduces rounding errors by treating the groups separately, leading to an incorrect result.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method
1. Total bears = 4 + 10 = 14.  
2. Divide total bears by bears per shelf: \( 14 \div 7 = 2 \).  
3. No rounding is needed since the division is exact.  
   **Result:** 2 shelves.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy
- Verify: \( 2 \times 7 = 14 \), which matches the total number of bears. ✅  
- No bears are left unaccounted for, and the calculation is consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **2**.
EXTRACTED:
2.0

Q:  At lunch a waiter had 9 customers and 5 of them didn't leave a tip. If he got $8 each from the ones who did tip, how much money did he earn? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to calculate the total earnings of a waiter based on tips received from customers. Out of 9 customers, only 4 left tips, and each tip was $8. The strategy involves determining the number of tipping customers and multiplying by the tip amount.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total customers: 9
- Customers who didn't tip: 5
- Tip amount per tipping customer: $8
- Assumption: All tipping customers left exactly $8.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
Subtract the number of non-tipping customers from the total number of customers to find the number of tipping customers. Then multiply the number of tipping customers by the tip amount.

**Method 2:**  
Directly calculate the total earnings by summing $8 for each tipping customer. This involves explicitly listing the tips for each tipping customer and adding them together.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Number of tipping customers = Total customers - Non-tipping customers  
   \( 9 - 5 = 4 \)
2. Total earnings = Number of tipping customers × Tip amount  
   \( 4 \times 8 = 32 \)

#### **Method 2:**
1. List the tips for each tipping customer: $8, $8, $8, $8.
2. Add the tips together:  
   \( 8 + 8 + 8 + 8 = 32 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is more efficient and avoids repetitive addition. It uses subtraction and multiplication, which are faster and less prone to error for larger numbers.
- **Method 2** is valid but unnecessarily tedious for this problem.

Preferred method: **Method 1**

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Number of tipping customers = \( 9 - 5 = 4 \)
2. Total earnings = \( 4 \times 8 = 32 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse calculation: Divide total earnings by tip amount to confirm the number of tipping customers:  
  \( 32 \div 8 = 4 \), which matches the number of tipping customers.
- Verify addition: \( 8 + 8 + 8 + 8 = 32 \), consistent with the result.

---

### Final Answer:
The waiter earned $32.  
The answer is **32**.

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many shelves are needed to store a total number of stuffed bears, given that each shelf holds 6 bears. First, we calculate the total number of bears, then divide this total by the shelf capacity to find the number of shelves required. If the division results in a remainder, we round up to ensure all bears are stored.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial stock of bears: 5
- Additional shipment: 7
- Shelf capacity: 6 bears per shelf
- Assumption: All bears must be stored, and no bears are left out.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct calculation using division.
1. Add the initial stock and the shipment to find the total number of bears.
2. Divide the total number of bears by the shelf capacity.
3. If there is a remainder, round up to the nearest whole number.

#### Method 2: Incremental grouping.
1. Start with the total number of bears and repeatedly subtract the shelf capacity (6 bears per shelf) until the remaining bears are fewer than 6.
2. Count the number of subtractions to determine the number of shelves.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct calculation using division.
1. Total bears = Initial stock + Shipment = 5 + 7 = 12.
2. Divide total bears by shelf capacity: \( 12 \div 6 = 2 \) (no remainder).
3. Since there is no remainder, exactly 2 shelves are needed.

#### Method 2: Incremental grouping.
1. Start with 12 bears.
2. Subtract 6 bears (1 shelf used): Remaining bears = \( 12 - 6 = 6 \).
3. Subtract another 6 bears (2nd shelf used): Remaining bears = \( 6 - 6 = 0 \).
4. No bears remain, so the total number of shelves used is 2.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient, as it uses division directly to calculate the number of shelves. It avoids the repetitive subtraction process of Method 2.
- **Method 2** is valid but less efficient for larger numbers, as it involves multiple steps of subtraction.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total bears = \( 5 + 7 = 12 \).
2. Divide total bears by shelf capacity: \( 12 \div 6 = 2 \).
3. No remainder, so exactly 2 shelves are needed.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify: \( 2 \times 6 = 12 \), which matches the total number of bears.
- No bears are left out, confirming the calculation is correct.

---

### Final Answer:
The toy store used 2 shelves to store the bears.  
**The answer is 2.**

To determine how much she spends, we need to calculate the total number of flowers purchased and multiply by the price per flower. ---

- Number of roses = 7
- Number of daisies = 3

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 2:**

2. Cost of daisies = 3 daisies × $6 = $18.
3. Total cost = $42 + $18 = $60.

- Total flowers = 7 + 3 = 10.

Q:  Nancy and her mom were picking carrots from their garden. Nancy picked 38 and her mother picked 47. If only 71 of the carrots were good, how many bad carrots did they have? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the number of bad carrots. Nancy and her mom picked a total number of carrots, but only 71 of them were good. The bad carrots are the difference between the total number of carrots picked and the number of good carrots. This requires summing the carrots picked by Nancy and her mom, then subtracting the good carrots.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Nancy picked 38 carrots.
- Nancy's mom picked 47 carrots.
- Total good carrots = 71.
- All carrots picked are either good or bad (no other categories).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the carrots picked by Nancy and her mom to find the total number of carrots picked.  
2. Subtract the number of good carrots from the total to find the number of bad carrots.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the number of good carrots directly from the carrots picked by each person, then sum the bad carrots from both individuals.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### Method 1:
1. Total carrots picked = 38 (Nancy) + 47 (Mom) = 85.  
2. Bad carrots = Total carrots picked - Good carrots = 85 - 71 = 14.

#### Method 2:
1. Bad carrots from Nancy = 38 - (Nancy's share of good carrots).  
   - Assume good carrots are distributed proportionally. Nancy picked \( \frac{38}{85} \) of the total carrots, so her share of good carrots is \( \frac{38}{85} \times 71 \approx 31.76 \).  
   - Bad carrots from Nancy = 38 - 31.76 ≈ 6.24.  
2. Bad carrots from Mom = 47 - (Mom's share of good carrots).  
   - Mom picked \( \frac{47}{85} \) of the total carrots, so her share of good carrots is \( \frac{47}{85} \times 71 \approx 39.24 \).  
   - Bad carrots from Mom = 47 - 39.24 ≈ 7.76.  
3. Total bad carrots = 6.24 + 7.76 ≈ 14.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is simpler and avoids unnecessary proportional calculations. It directly computes the total bad carrots by subtracting the good carrots from the total picked.  
- **Method 2** is more complex and introduces rounding errors due to proportional distribution, making it less efficient and less accurate.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total carrots picked = 38 + 47 = 85.  
2. Bad carrots = Total carrots picked - Good carrots = 85 - 71 = 14.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
1. Total carrots picked = 38 + 47 = 85.  
2. Good carrots = 71.  
3. Bad carrots = 85 - 71 = 14.  
4. Verify: Good carrots (71) + Bad carrots (14) = Total carrots (85). ✅

---

The answer is **14**.
EXTRACTED:
14.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
14.0

Q:  Isabel picked 66 flowers for her friend’s wedding. She was making bouquets with 8 flowers in each one. If 10 of the flowers wilted before the wedding, how many bouquets could she still make? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many bouquets Isabel can make after accounting for wilted flowers. First, subtract the wilted flowers from the total number of flowers to find the usable flowers. Then divide the usable flowers by the number of flowers per bouquet (8) to calculate the number of complete bouquets she can make. This involves subtraction and division.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total flowers picked: 66
- Wilted flowers: 10
- Flowers per bouquet: 8
- Only complete bouquets can be made (no partial bouquets).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
Subtract the wilted flowers from the total flowers to find the usable flowers. Then divide the usable flowers by 8 and take the integer part of the result (floor division).

**Method 2:**  
First, calculate how many flowers are lost due to wilting and determine the remainder of usable flowers. Then repeatedly subtract 8 flowers (one bouquet at a time) until fewer than 8 flowers remain. Count the number of subtractions to find the number of bouquets.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction and division**
1. Subtract wilted flowers:  
   \( 66 - 10 = 56 \) usable flowers.
2. Divide usable flowers by 8:  
   \( 56 \div 8 = 7 \) complete bouquets.

---

#### **Method 2: Iterative subtraction**
1. Start with 56 usable flowers (after subtracting wilted flowers).  
2. Subtract 8 flowers repeatedly:  
   - \( 56 - 8 = 48 \) (1 bouquet)  
   - \( 48 - 8 = 40 \) (2 bouquets)  
   - \( 40 - 8 = 32 \) (3 bouquets)  
   - \( 32 - 8 = 24 \) (4 bouquets)  
   - \( 24 - 8 = 16 \) (5 bouquets)  
   - \( 16 - 8 = 8 \) (6 bouquets)  
   - \( 8 - 8 = 0 \) (7 bouquets).  
3. Count the number of subtractions: 7 bouquets.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result (7 bouquets). However, **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses direct arithmetic operations (subtraction and division) rather than iterative subtraction. Method 2 is valid but unnecessarily time-consuming.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Subtract wilted flowers:  
   \( 66 - 10 = 56 \) usable flowers.
2. Divide usable flowers by 8:  
   \( 56 \div 8 = 7 \) complete bouquets.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify by multiplication: \( 7 \times 8 = 56 \) flowers used for bouquets.  
- Remaining flowers: \( 66 - 10 = 56 \), and all 56 flowers are used.  
- No flowers are left over, confirming the calculation.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **7**.
EXTRACTED:
7.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
7.0

Q:  A pet store has 9 bird cages. If each cage has 2 parrots and 6 parakeets in it, how many birds does the pet store have total? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the total number of birds in a pet store. Each bird cage contains 2 parrots and 6 parakeets, and there are 9 cages in total. To solve this, we need to calculate the total number of birds in one cage and then multiply that by the number of cages.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Number of cages = 9
- Each cage contains:
  - Parrots = 2
  - Parakeets = 6
- Total birds in one cage = Parrots + Parakeets = 2 + 6 = 8
- All cages are identical, and no birds are missing or unaccounted for.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1 (Direct Calculation):** First, calculate the total number of birds in one cage (2 parrots + 6 parakeets = 8 birds). Then multiply the total number of birds in one cage by the number of cages (9).
2. **Method 2 (Separate Totals):** Calculate the total number of parrots across all cages (2 parrots × 9 cages) and the total number of parakeets across all cages (6 parakeets × 9 cages). Add these two totals to find the overall number of birds.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1 (Direct Calculation):
1. Total birds in one cage = 2 parrots + 6 parakeets = 8 birds.
2. Total birds in all cages = 8 birds/cage × 9 cages = 72 birds.

#### Method 2 (Separate Totals):
1. Total parrots = 2 parrots/cage × 9 cages = 18 parrots.
2. Total parakeets = 6 parakeets/cage × 9 cages = 54 parakeets.
3. Total birds = Total parrots + Total parakeets = 18 + 54 = 72 birds.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result (72 birds). 
- **Method 1** is simpler and more direct because it combines the bird counts in one step before multiplying by the number of cages. This reduces the number of intermediate calculations and is less prone to error.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Total birds in one cage = 2 + 6 = 8.
2. Total birds in all cages = 8 × 9 = 72.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result:
- Using Method 2: Total parrots = 18, Total parakeets = 54, Total birds = 18 + 54 = 72 ✅.
- Reverse calculation: 72 birds ÷ 9 cages = 8 birds/cage, which matches the initial calculation of 2 parrots + 6 parakeets = 8 birds/cage ✅.

---

The answer is **72**.
EXTRACTED:
72.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
72.0

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Henry has 3 action figures but needs 8 total for his collection. He must buy the remaining 5 action figures, and each costs $6. To find the total cost, we calculate the number of figures he needs to buy and multiply by the price per figure.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total action figures needed: 8
- Action figures Henry already owns: 3
- Action figures Henry needs to buy: \( 8 - 3 = 5 \)
- Cost per action figure: $6
- No discounts or additional costs are applied.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
Calculate the number of action figures Henry needs to buy, then multiply by the cost per figure. This method directly computes the total cost.

**Method 2:**  
First calculate the total cost for 8 action figures, then subtract the cost of the 3 figures Henry already owns. This method uses subtraction to find the cost of the remaining figures.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the number of action figures Henry needs to buy:  
   \( 8 - 3 = 5 \)
2. Multiply the number of figures by the cost per figure:  
   \( 5 \times 6 = 30 \)

#### **Method 2:**
1. Calculate the total cost for 8 action figures:  
   \( 8 \times 6 = 48 \)
2. Subtract the cost of the 3 figures Henry already owns:  
   \( 48 - (3 \times 6) = 48 - 18 = 30 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more direct and avoids unnecessary intermediate calculations. It is simpler and more efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Calculate the number of action figures Henry needs to buy:  
   \( 8 - 3 = 5 \)
2. Multiply the number of figures by the cost per figure:  
   \( 5 \times 6 = 30 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify using addition: \( 3 \times 6 = 18 \), \( 5 \times 6 = 30 \), and \( 18 + 30 = 48 \). The total cost for 8 figures matches expectations. ✅
- Reverse calculation: \( 30 \div 6 = 5 \), confirming the number of figures Henry needs to buy. ✅

---

The answer is **30**.

Q:  While shopping, Maria bought 40 green towels and 44 white towels. If she gave her mother 65 of them, how many towels did Maria end up with? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Maria bought a total of green and white towels, then gave away some to her mother. We need to calculate how many towels Maria has left. This involves summing the initial quantities of towels and subtracting the number given away.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Green towels: 40
- White towels: 44
- Towels given to her mother: 65
- No towels lost or damaged during the process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the green and white towels to find the total number of towels Maria initially had.  
2. Subtract the number of towels given to her mother from the total.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the towels given to her mother from the green towels first.  
2. If the green towels are insufficient, subtract the remaining amount from the white towels.  
3. Sum the remaining towels.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total towels = Green towels + White towels = 40 + 44 = 84.  
2. Towels left = Total towels - Towels given to her mother = 84 - 65 = 19.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Start with green towels: 40 - 65 = -25 (not enough green towels).  
2. Use white towels to cover the deficit: White towels = 44 - 25 = 19.  
3. Remaining towels = 19.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is simpler and avoids unnecessary intermediate steps. It directly calculates the total towels and subtracts the given amount.  
- **Method 2** is valid but introduces unnecessary complexity by splitting the subtraction across towel types.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total towels = 40 + 44 = 84.  
2. Towels left = 84 - 65 = 19.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse calculation: Towels left + Towels given to her mother = 19 + 65 = 84 (matches the total towels). ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **19**.
