RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many $9 games Will can buy after spending $41 on mower blades. This involves two steps: first, calculate how much money Will has left after his purchase, and second, determine how many $9 games he can afford by dividing the remaining money by 9 and taking the integer part of the result.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total money earned: $104
- Money spent on mower blades: $41
- Cost of each game: $9
- Assumptions: Will spends only whole dollars on games, and any leftover money that is less than $9 cannot be used to buy another game.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
1. Subtract the cost of the mower blades from the total money earned to find the remaining money.  
2. Divide the remaining money by 9 and take the integer part of the result to find the number of games Will can buy.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the cost of the mower blades from the total money earned to find the remaining money.  
2. Use repeated subtraction of $9 from the remaining money to count how many games Will can afford.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Subtract the cost of the mower blades:  
   \( 104 - 41 = 63 \) (remaining money).  
2. Divide the remaining money by 9:  
   \( 63 \div 9 = 7 \).  
   Will can buy 7 games.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Subtract the cost of the mower blades:  
   \( 104 - 41 = 63 \) (remaining money).  
2. Repeatedly subtract $9 from $63 and count the number of subtractions:  
   - \( 63 - 9 = 54 \) (1 game)  
   - \( 54 - 9 = 45 \) (2 games)  
   - \( 45 - 9 = 36 \) (3 games)  
   - \( 36 - 9 = 27 \) (4 games)  
   - \( 27 - 9 = 18 \) (5 games)  
   - \( 18 - 9 = 9 \) (6 games)  
   - \( 9 - 9 = 0 \) (7 games).  
   Will can buy 7 games.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses division instead of repeated subtraction, which is computationally simpler and faster.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Subtract the cost of the mower blades:  
   \( 104 - 41 = 63 \).  
2. Divide the remaining money by 9:  
   \( 63 \div 9 = 7 \).  
   Will can buy 7 games.

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
1. Total money spent on games: \( 7 \times 9 = 63 \).  
2. Remaining money after buying games: \( 63 - 63 = 0 \).  
3. Total money accounted for: \( 41 + 63 = 104 \), which matches the initial amount. ✅

---

The answer is **7**.

Q:  A new building needed 11 windows. The builder had already installed 4 of them. If it takes 8 hours to install each window, how long will it take him to install the rest? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine the total time required to install the remaining windows in a building. First, we calculate how many windows are left to be installed, then multiply that number by the time it takes to install each window (8 hours). This involves subtraction and multiplication.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total windows needed: 11
- Windows already installed: 4
- Time to install each window: 8 hours
- Assumption: The installation time per window is constant, and no interruptions occur.

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
1. Subtract the number of installed windows from the total to find the remaining windows.  
2. Multiply the remaining windows by the time per window to find the total time.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the total time required to install all 11 windows.  
2. Subtract the time already spent installing the 4 completed windows to find the time for the remaining windows.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Remaining windows = Total windows - Installed windows = \( 11 - 4 = 7 \).
2. Total time = Remaining windows × Time per window = \( 7 \times 8 = 56 \) hours.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Total time for all windows = Total windows × Time per window = \( 11 \times 8 = 88 \) hours.
2. Time already spent = Installed windows × Time per window = \( 4 \times 8 = 32 \) hours.
3. Time for remaining windows = Total time - Time already spent = \( 88 - 32 = 56 \) hours.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more direct and involves fewer steps, making it the preferred approach.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method:
1. Remaining windows = \( 11 - 4 = 7 \).
2. Total time = \( 7 \times 8 = 56 \) hours.

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
- Verify by reversing the calculation: \( 56 \div 8 = 7 \) (remaining windows), and \( 7 + 4 = 11 \) (total windows). ✅
- Cross-check with Method 2: \( 88 - 32 = 56 \). ✅

---

The answer is **56 hours**.

56.0

Q:  Frank was collecting cans for recycling. On Saturday he filled 5 bags up and on Sunday he filled 3 more bags. If each bag had 5 cans in it, how many cans did he pick up total? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the total number of cans Frank collected over two days. First, we calculate the total number of bags filled on both days, then multiply the total number of bags by the number of cans per bag. This involves basic addition and multiplication.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- On Saturday, Frank filled **5 bags**.
- On Sunday, Frank filled **3 bags**.
- Each bag contains **5 cans**.
- No bags were partially filled or lost.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the number of bags filled on Saturday and Sunday to get the total number of bags.  
2. Multiply the total number of bags by the number of cans per bag.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the number of cans collected on Saturday by multiplying the number of bags filled on Saturday by the number of cans per bag.  
2. Calculate the number of cans collected on Sunday in the same way.  
3. Add the results from Saturday and Sunday to get the total number of cans.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### Method 1:
1. Total bags = 5 (Saturday) + 3 (Sunday) = **8 bags**.  
2. Total cans = 8 bags × 5 cans per bag = **40 cans**.

#### Method 2:
1. Cans collected on Saturday = 5 bags × 5 cans per bag = **25 cans**.  
2. Cans collected on Sunday = 3 bags × 5 cans per bag = **15 cans**.  
3. Total cans = 25 cans + 15 cans = **40 cans**.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it combines the total number of bags first and then performs a single multiplication.  
- **Method 2** is slightly longer but equally valid, as it calculates the cans for each day separately before summing them.  

Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is preferred for its simplicity.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
1. Total bags = 5 + 3 = **8 bags**.  
2. Total cans = 8 × 5 = **40 cans**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Reverse-check: If there are 40 cans total and each bag contains 5 cans, then 40 ÷ 5 = 8 bags. This matches the total number of bags (5 + 3 = 8). ✅  
- Cross-check with Method 2: 25 (Saturday) + 15 (Sunday) = 40 cans. ✅  

---

The answer is **40**.

### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many oranges remain in the bin after 37 old oranges are thrown away and 7 new oranges are added. This involves subtracting the number of old oranges removed from the initial count and then adding the new oranges. A direct subtraction and addition approach should suffice.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial number of oranges: 40
- Old oranges thrown away: 37
- New oranges added: 7
- No other changes to the bin (e.g., no additional removals or additions).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Sequential operations: First subtract the number of old oranges thrown away from the initial count, then add the new oranges to the result.

**Method 2:** Combine the operations into a single equation: Subtract the old oranges and add the new oranges simultaneously.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Sequential operations**
1. Start with the initial count: 40 oranges.
2. Subtract the old oranges thrown away: \( 40 - 37 = 3 \).
3. Add the new oranges: \( 3 + 7 = 10 \).

Result from Method 1: 10 oranges.

---

#### **Method 2: Combined operations**
1. Use a single equation: \( 40 - 37 + 7 \).
2. Perform subtraction first: \( 40 - 37 = 3 \).
3. Add the new oranges: \( 3 + 7 = 10 \).

Result from Method 2: 10 oranges.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more efficient because it combines the operations into a single equation, reducing intermediate steps. Method 1 is valid but slightly more cumbersome.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
Using Method 2:
\[
40 - 37 + 7 = 3 + 7 = 10
\]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Start with 40 oranges.
- Remove 37 old oranges: \( 40 - 37 = 3 \).
- Add 7 new oranges: \( 3 + 7 = 10 \).
The calculations are consistent and accurate.

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the total points scored by a trivia team during a game. To do this, we need to determine how many members showed up, then calculate the total points scored by multiplying the number of attendees by the points each scored. A straightforward approach involves subtraction and multiplication.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total team members = 9
- Members who didn’t show up = 3
- Members who did show up = Total members - Members who didn’t show up
- Points scored per attending member = 2
- Total points = (Number of attendees) × (Points per attendee)

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
Subtract the number of absent members from the total team members to find the number of attendees. Then multiply the number of attendees by the points scored per attendee.

**Method 2:**  
Directly calculate the total points by first determining the number of attendees and then summing their individual contributions (2 points each) iteratively.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Calculate the number of attendees:  
   \( 9 - 3 = 6 \)
2. Calculate the total points scored:  
   \( 6 \times 2 = 12 \)

#### **Method 2:**
1. Determine the number of attendees:  
   \( 9 - 3 = 6 \)
2. Add the points scored by each attendee iteratively:  
   \( 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 + 2 = 12 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
- **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses direct multiplication, which is faster and less error-prone than iterative addition.
- **Method 2** is valid but unnecessarily lengthy for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Number of attendees:  
   \( 9 - 3 = 6 \)
2. Total points scored:  
   \( 6 \times 2 = 12 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
- Reverse the calculation:  
  Total points \( \div \) Points per attendee = Number of attendees  
  \( 12 \div 2 = 6 \), which matches the number of attendees.  
- Add attendees and absentees:  
  \( 6 + 3 = 9 \), which matches the total team members.

---

The answer is **12**.

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many students are in each group after dividing the students who were picked for the trivia teams into 3 equal groups. First, we calculate how many students were picked by subtracting the number of students who were not picked from the total number of students. Then, we divide the remaining students equally among the 3 groups. The strategy involves subtraction followed by division.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total students trying out: 17
- Students not picked: 5
- Students picked = Total students - Students not picked = \( 17 - 5 \)
- The picked students are divided equally into 3 groups.
- Assumption: The division results in an exact whole number (no remainder).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
1. Subtract the number of students not picked from the total to find the number of students picked.  
2. Divide the number of picked students by 3 to find the number of students in each group.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the total number of students picked as a fraction of the total students: \( \frac{17 - 5}{3} \).  
2. Simplify the fraction to find the number of students in each group directly.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Subtract the students not picked:  
   \( 17 - 5 = 12 \) (students picked).  
2. Divide the picked students into 3 groups:  
   \( 12 \div 3 = 4 \) (students per group).

#### **Method 2:**
1. Express the calculation as a fraction:  
   \( \frac{17 - 5}{3} = \frac{12}{3} \).  
2. Simplify the fraction:  
   \( \frac{12}{3} = 4 \) (students per group).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more intuitive and easier to follow because it separates the subtraction and division into clear steps. **Method 2** is slightly more abstract and less practical for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Subtract the students not picked:  
   \( 17 - 5 = 12 \).  
2. Divide the picked students into 3 groups:  
   \( 12 \div 3 = 4 \).

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:

- Dividing 12 students into 3 groups: \( 12 \div 3 = 4 \).

Q:  John earned 8 dollars for each lawn he mowed. If he had 15 lawns to mow, but forgot to mow 7 of them, how much money did he actually earn? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with calculating how much money John actually earned by mowing lawns. He earns $8 per lawn, but he only mowed a portion of the total lawns assigned. The strategy involves determining how many lawns he actually mowed, then multiplying that number by his earnings per lawn.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- John earns $8 per lawn.
- Total lawns assigned = 15.
- Lawns not mowed = 7.
- Lawns actually mowed = Total lawns - Lawns not mowed.
- No additional bonuses or deductions are mentioned.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1 (Direct subtraction):** Subtract the number of lawns not mowed from the total lawns assigned to find the number of lawns mowed. Then multiply this result by $8.
2. **Method 2 (Complementary approach):** Calculate the total possible earnings if all 15 lawns were mowed, then subtract the earnings John missed by not mowing 7 lawns.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1 (Direct subtraction):
1. Calculate the number of lawns mowed:
   \[
   \text{Lawns mowed} = 15 - 7 = 8
   \]
2. Calculate the total earnings:
   \[
   \text{Earnings} = 8 \times 8 = 64
   \]

#### Method 2 (Complementary approach):
1. Calculate the total possible earnings if all 15 lawns were mowed:
   \[
   \text{Total possible earnings} = 15 \times 8 = 120
   \]
2. Calculate the earnings missed by not mowing 7 lawns:
   \[
   \text{Missed earnings} = 7 \times 8 = 56
   \]
3. Subtract the missed earnings from the total possible earnings:
   \[
   \text{Earnings} = 120 - 56 = 64
   \]

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more direct and involves fewer steps. It is the preferred method for simplicity and efficiency.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Calculate the number of lawns mowed:
   \[
   \text{Lawns mowed} = 15 - 7 = 8
   \]
2. Calculate the total earnings:
   \[
   \text{Earnings} = 8 \times 8 = 64
   \]

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify using Method 2:
  \[
  \text{Total possible earnings} = 15 \times 8 = 120
  \]
  \[
  \text{Missed earnings} = 7 \times 8 = 56
  \]
  \[
  \text{Earnings} = 120 - 56 = 64
  \]
- Both methods confirm the result.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **64**.

We are tasked with finding the number of homework problems on each page. Sarah started with 55 problems, completed 6, and still has 7 pages of problems left. Since each page has the same number of problems, we need to divide the remaining problems evenly across the 7 pages. This involves subtraction to find the remaining problems and division to determine the number of problems per page.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total homework problems: 55
- Problems completed: 6
- Remaining pages: 7
- Each page has the same number of problems.
- No problems are missing or incomplete.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
Subtract the completed problems from the total to find the remaining problems, then divide the remaining problems by the number of pages.

**Method 2:**  
Divide the total number of problems (55) by the total number of pages (7), then subtract the fraction of problems completed (6 ÷ 7) to adjust for the completed problems.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Subtract completed problems:  
   \( 55 - 6 = 49 \) (remaining problems).
2. Divide remaining problems by the number of pages:  
   \( 49 \div 7 = 7 \) (problems per page).

#### **Method 2:**
1. Divide total problems by the number of pages:  
   \( 55 \div 7 = 7.857 \) (problems per page before accounting for completed problems).  
2. Subtract the fraction of completed problems:  
   \( 7.857 - (6 \div 7) = 7.857 - 0.857 = 7 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is simpler and avoids unnecessary fractional calculations. It directly uses subtraction and division, which are straightforward operations for this problem.
- **Method 2** introduces unnecessary complexity by dividing the total problems first and then adjusting for completed problems, which is less intuitive.

Preferred method: **Method 1**.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Subtract completed problems:  
   \( 55 - 6 = 49 \).
2. Divide remaining problems by the number of pages:  
   \( 49 \div 7 = 7 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Multiply the number of problems per page by the number of pages:  
  \( 7 \times 7 = 49 \) (remaining problems).  
- Add completed problems:  
  \( 49 + 6 = 55 \) (total problems matches the original count). ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The number of problems on each page is **7**.  
The answer is **7**.
EXTRACTED:
7.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
7.0

Q:  Roger had 16 dollars. For his birthday he got 28 more dollars but spent 25 on a new game. How much money does he have now? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Roger starts with $16, receives $28 for his birthday, and spends $25 on a new game. To find how much money he has now, we need to calculate the total money he had after receiving the birthday gift, subtract the amount spent, and verify the result. This involves addition and subtraction.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial money: $16
- Birthday gift: $28
- Money spent: $25
- No other transactions or changes in money.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Sequential calculation: Add the birthday gift to the initial amount, then subtract the money spent.  
**Method 2:** Combine the birthday gift and the amount spent first (net change), then add this result to the initial amount.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation**
1. Add the birthday gift to the initial amount:  
   \( 16 + 28 = 44 \)
2. Subtract the money spent:  
   \( 44 - 25 = 19 \)

#### **Method 2: Combine net change first**
1. Calculate the net change from the birthday gift and money spent:  
   \( 28 - 25 = 3 \)
2. Add the net change to the initial amount:  
   \( 16 + 3 = 19 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more intuitive because it follows the sequence of events (adding the gift first, then subtracting the expense). **Method 2** is valid but less straightforward for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method:
1. Add the birthday gift to the initial amount:  
   \( 16 + 28 = 44 \)
2. Subtract the money spent:  
   \( 44 - 25 = 19 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse-check: Start with the final amount ($19), add the money spent ($25), and verify the total before spending ($44). Subtract the birthday gift ($28) to confirm the initial amount ($16).  
  \( 19 + 25 = 44 \), \( 44 - 28 = 16 \) ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **19**.
EXTRACTED:
19.0
GROUND_TRUTH:

Q:  While playing a trivia game, Mike answered 3 questions correct in the first half and 5 questions correct in the second half. If each question was worth 3 points, what was his final score? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with calculating Mike's final score in a trivia game. Each correct answer is worth 3 points, and Mike answered 3 questions correctly in the first half and 5 questions correctly in the second half. To find the total score, we need to compute the total number of correct answers and multiply it by the points per question.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Correct answers in the first half: 3
- Correct answers in the second half: 5
- Points per correct answer: 3
- Assumption: All questions are scored equally, and there are no penalties for incorrect answers.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the number of correct answers from both halves.  
2. Multiply the total number of correct answers by the points per question.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the score for each half separately by multiplying the number of correct answers in each half by the points per question.  
2. Add the scores from both halves to get the total score.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total correct answers = 3 (first half) + 5 (second half) = 8.  
2. Total score = 8 × 3 = 24.

#### **Method 2:**
1. First half score = 3 × 3 = 9.  
2. Second half score = 5 × 3 = 15.  
3. Total score = 9 + 15 = 24.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more efficient because it combines the total correct answers first and performs a single multiplication. **Method 2** involves more intermediate steps but is equally valid.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total correct answers = 3 + 5 = 8.  
2. Total score = 8 × 3 = 24.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Using Method 2: First half score = 9, second half score = 15, total score = 9 + 15 = 24. ✅  
- Reverse calculation: Total score ÷ points per question = 24 ÷ 3 = 8 correct answers. ✅

---

The answer is **24**.
EXTRACTED:
24.0
GROUND_TRUTH:

Q:  A pet store had 56 puppies. In one day they sold 24 of them and put the rest into cages with 4 in each cage. How many cages did they use? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many cages are needed to house the remaining puppies after some are sold. First, we calculate the number of puppies left after sales. Then, we divide this number by the capacity of each cage (4 puppies per cage). The goal is to find the total number of cages required.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total puppies initially: 56
- Puppies sold: 24
- Puppies per cage: 4
- Remaining puppies will be evenly distributed into cages.
- No partial cages are allowed; if there are leftover puppies, an additional cage is needed.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Sequential calculation
1. Subtract the number of puppies sold from the total to find the remaining puppies.
2. Divide the remaining puppies by the cage capacity (4 puppies per cage) and round up if there is a remainder.

#### Method 2: Combine operations
1. Use a single formula: \((\text{Total puppies} - \text{Sold puppies}) \div \text{Puppies per cage}\), and round up the result to account for any remainder.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Sequential calculation
1. Calculate remaining puppies: \(56 - 24 = 32\).
2. Divide remaining puppies by cage capacity: \(32 \div 4 = 8\).
3. Since there is no remainder, exactly 8 cages are needed.

#### Method 2: Combine operations
1. Use the formula: \((56 - 24) \div 4 = 32 \div 4 = 8\).
2. No rounding is needed because the division is exact.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result and are equally valid.
- Method 2 is slightly more efficient because it combines operations into a single step, reducing intermediate calculations.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Use the formula: \((56 - 24) \div 4 = 32 \div 4 = 8\).
2. No rounding is needed because the division is exact.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
1. Verify remaining puppies: \(56 - 24 = 32\).
2. Verify cage calculation: \(32 \div 4 = 8\).
3. Confirm no remainder: \(32 \mod 4 = 0\).

---

### Final Answer:
The pet store used **8 cages**.  
The answer is **8**.

8.0

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Wendy assembled 4 chairs and 4 tables, spending 6 minutes on each piece of furniture. To find the total time she spent, we need to calculate the total number of furniture pieces and multiply it by the time spent per piece. This involves basic multiplication and addition.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Number of chairs: 4
- Number of tables: 4
- Time spent per piece of furniture: 6 minutes
- Total furniture = chairs + tables = 4 + 4 = 8 pieces
- No interruptions or variations in assembly time.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1 (Direct Multiplication):** Add the number of chairs and tables to get the total number of furniture pieces, then multiply by the time spent per piece.
2. **Method 2 (Separate Calculations):** Calculate the total time spent on chairs and the total time spent on tables separately, then add the results.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1 (Direct Multiplication):
1. Total furniture = 4 chairs + 4 tables = 8 pieces.
2. Total time = 8 pieces × 6 minutes per piece = 48 minutes.

#### Method 2 (Separate Calculations):
1. Time spent on chairs = 4 chairs × 6 minutes per chair = 24 minutes.
2. Time spent on tables = 4 tables × 6 minutes per table = 24 minutes.
3. Total time = 24 minutes (chairs) + 24 minutes (tables) = 48 minutes.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result (48 minutes).
- **Method 1** is more efficient because it combines the calculations into a single step, avoiding intermediate steps.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total furniture = 4 chairs + 4 tables = 8 pieces.
2. Total time = 8 pieces × 6 minutes per piece = 48 minutes.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify by reverse calculation: 48 minutes ÷ 6 minutes per piece = 8 pieces (4 chairs + 4 tables). ✅
- Verify using Method 2: 24 minutes (chairs) + 24 minutes (tables) = 48 minutes. ✅

---

The answer is **48 minutes**.

Q:  Maria was making baggies of cookies with 5 cookies in each bag. If she had 33 chocolate chip cookies and 2 oatmeal cookies, how many baggies could she make? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Maria is dividing her cookies into baggies, with 5 cookies per bag. We need to determine how many complete baggies she can make using the total number of cookies she has. This involves summing the total number of cookies, dividing by 5, and considering only the whole number of baggies (ignoring any leftover cookies).

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Maria has **33 chocolate chip cookies** and **2 oatmeal cookies**.
- Each baggie contains **5 cookies**.
- Only complete baggies can be made (no partial baggies).

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods:
1. **Method 1: Direct addition and division**  
   Add the total number of cookies, then divide by 5 to find the number of complete baggies.

2. **Method 2: Separate grouping and summation**  
   Group the chocolate chip cookies into baggies first, then group the oatmeal cookies, and sum the total number of baggies.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### Method 1: Direct addition and division
1. Total cookies = 33 (chocolate chip) + 2 (oatmeal) = **35 cookies**.
2. Divide total cookies by 5:  
   \( 35 \div 5 = 7 \) complete baggies.

#### Method 2: Separate grouping and summation
1. Group the chocolate chip cookies:  
   \( 33 \div 5 = 6 \) complete baggies (with 3 cookies left over).
2. Group the oatmeal cookies:  
   \( 2 \div 5 = 0 \) complete baggies (with 2 cookies left over).
3. Total complete baggies = \( 6 + 0 = 7 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods:
- Both methods yield the same result: **7 complete baggies**.
- Method 1 is more direct and efficient because it avoids unnecessary intermediate steps.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Total cookies = \( 33 + 2 = 35 \).
2. Divide by 5: \( 35 \div 5 = 7 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
- Verify by multiplying the number of baggies by the number of cookies per bag:  
  \( 7 \times 5 = 35 \), which matches the total number of cookies. ✅

---

The answer is **7**.

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with calculating the total number of winter clothing items Paige has. Each box contains scarves and mittens, and there are multiple boxes. The strategy involves determining the total number of items per box and multiplying by the number of boxes.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Number of boxes: 6
- Scarves per box: 5
- Mittens per box: 5
- Total items per box = scarves + mittens = 5 + 5 = 10
- All boxes contain the same number of items.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Calculate the total items per box first, then multiply by the number of boxes.  
**Method 2:** Calculate the total scarves and total mittens separately, then add them together.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total items per box = 5 scarves + 5 mittens = 10 items.
2. Total items across all boxes = 10 items/box × 6 boxes = 60 items.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Total scarves = 5 scarves/box × 6 boxes = 30 scarves.
2. Total mittens = 5 mittens/box × 6 boxes = 30 mittens.
3. Total items = 30 scarves + 30 mittens = 60 items.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it combines the calculation of scarves and mittens into a single step before multiplying by the number of boxes. **Method 2** involves more intermediate steps, which are unnecessary for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total items per box = 5 scarves + 5 mittens = 10 items.
2. Total items across all boxes = 10 items/box × 6 boxes = 60 items.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify using Method 2: Total scarves = 30, total mittens = 30, total items = 30 + 30 = 60 ✅
- Verify logic: Each box contains 10 items, and there are 6 boxes, so 10 × 6 = 60 ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **60**.

Q:  For the school bake sale Maria made 19 cupcakes. If she sold 5 of them and then made 10 more, how many cupcakes would she have? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many cupcakes Maria has after selling some and then baking more. This involves subtracting the sold cupcakes from the initial batch and then adding the newly baked cupcakes. A step-by-step approach will ensure accuracy.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Maria initially made **19 cupcakes**.
- She sold **5 cupcakes**.
- She then made **10 more cupcakes**.
- No cupcakes were lost or discarded during the process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1 (Sequential Operations):** Subtract the sold cupcakes from the initial batch, then add the newly baked cupcakes to the remaining total.
2. **Method 2 (Net Change):** Combine the net change (subtract sold, add baked) into a single operation and apply it to the initial total.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### Method 1 (Sequential Operations):
1. Start with the initial number of cupcakes: **19**.
2. Subtract the sold cupcakes:  
   \( 19 - 5 = 14 \).
3. Add the newly baked cupcakes:  
   \( 14 + 10 = 24 \).

#### Method 2 (Net Change):
1. Combine the net change:  
   \( -5 + 10 = +5 \) (net increase of 5 cupcakes).
2. Apply the net change to the initial total:  
   \( 19 + 5 = 24 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result (**24 cupcakes**) and are equally valid.
- **Method 2** is more efficient because it combines the changes into a single operation, reducing the number of steps.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 2):
1. Calculate the net change:  
   \( -5 + 10 = +5 \).
2. Apply the net change to the initial total:  
   \( 19 + 5 = 24 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result:
- Reverse the process: Start with the final total (**24**), subtract the newly baked cupcakes (**10**), and add back the sold cupcakes (**5**):  
  \( 24 - 10 = 14 \), \( 14 + 5 = 19 \) (matches the initial total). ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **24**.
EXTRACTED:
24.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
24.0

Q:  Nancy's old washing machine could only wash 9 pieces of clothing at a time. If she had to wash 19 shirts and 8 sweaters how many loads would she have to do? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Nancy's washing machine can handle 9 pieces of clothing per load. She needs to wash 19 shirts and 8 sweaters, for a total of 19 + 8 = 27 pieces of clothing. The task is to determine how many loads are required to wash all 27 pieces, given the machine's capacity. This involves dividing the total number of items by the machine's capacity and rounding up to account for any leftover items.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total shirts: 19
- Total sweaters: 8
- Total clothing: \( 19 + 8 = 27 \)
- Washing machine capacity: 9 pieces per load
- Assumption: Each load must be full or partially full, and no items are left unwashed.

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
**Method 1: Direct division and rounding up**  
Divide the total number of clothing items by the machine's capacity. Since partial loads are not possible, round up to the nearest whole number.

**Method 2: Incremental grouping**  
Group the clothing into loads of 9 pieces each, counting how many full loads are needed. If there are leftover items after grouping, add one additional load.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### Method 1: Direct division and rounding up
1. Total clothing: \( 27 \)
2. Machine capacity: \( 9 \)
3. Compute \( 27 \div 9 = 3 \) (exact division, no remainder).
4. Since the result is a whole number, no rounding is needed. Nancy needs exactly 3 loads.

#### Method 2: Incremental grouping
1. Group the clothing into loads of 9:
   - First load: 9 items (27 - 9 = 18 remaining)
   - Second load: 9 items (18 - 9 = 9 remaining)
   - Third load: 9 items (9 - 9 = 0 remaining)
2. Total loads: 3.

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
Both methods yield the same result of 3 loads. However, **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses a single division operation and avoids manual grouping. Method 2 is more intuitive for visualizing the process but is less practical for larger numbers.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total clothing: \( 27 \)
2. Machine capacity: \( 9 \)
3. Compute \( 27 \div 9 = 3 \).
4. Nancy needs 3 loads.

---

### Step 6: Double-check
- Total clothing: \( 27 \)
- Machine capacity: \( 9 \)
- \( 9 \times 3 = 27 \), confirming that 3 loads are sufficient to wash all items.

---

The answer is **3**.

3.0

- Additional players: 2

- Then multiply the total number of players by the number of lives per player (4 × 6).

#### Method 2: Distributive property
- Multiply the lives for the initial 2 players (2 × 6).
- Multiply the lives for the additional 2 players (2 × 6).
- Add the results together.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct multiplication
1. Total number of players = 2 + 2 = 4.
2. Total lives = 4 × 6 = 24.

#### Method 2: Distributive property
1. Lives for initial players = 2 × 6 = 12.
2. Lives for additional players = 2 × 6 = 12.
3. Total lives = 12 + 12 = 24.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it combines the calculation into a single multiplication step after determining the total number of players.
- **Method 2** is valid but involves more intermediate steps, making it slightly less efficient.

Preferred method: **Method 1**.

   \( 10 + 6 = 16 \) pieces.

   \( 1 + 6 = 7 \) pieces.

Q:  The school cafeteria ordered 8 red apples and 43 green apples for students lunches. But, if only 42 students wanted fruit, how many extra did the cafeteria end up with? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many extra apples the cafeteria has after 42 students take one apple each. To solve this, we need to calculate the total number of apples ordered, subtract the number of apples taken by the students, and find the remainder. A direct subtraction or a step-by-step breakdown will work.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Red apples: 8
- Green apples: 43
- Total students wanting fruit: 42
- Each student takes exactly one apple.
- No apples are lost or wasted.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the total number of apples (red + green).  
2. Subtract the number of students (42) from the total apples to find the extra apples.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the number of students (42) from the green apples first.  
2. If there are still apples left, subtract the remainder from the red apples.  
3. Sum any leftover apples to find the total extra.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### **Method 1: Direct subtraction**
1. Total apples = 8 (red) + 43 (green) = 51 apples.  
2. Extra apples = Total apples - Students = 51 - 42 = 9 apples.

#### **Method 2: Step-by-step subtraction**
1. Start with green apples: 43 - 42 = 1 green apple left.  
2. Red apples remain untouched: 8 red apples.  
3. Total extra apples = 1 (green) + 8 (red) = 9 apples.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it calculates the total apples first and subtracts the students in one step.  
- **Method 2** is valid but involves unnecessary intermediate steps, making it less efficient.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total apples = 8 + 43 = 51.  
2. Extra apples = 51 - 42 = 9.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- If 42 students take one apple each, they take 42 apples in total.  
- Total apples = 51. Apples left = 51 - 42 = 9.  
- Rechecking with Method 2 confirms the same result.

---

The answer is **9**.

9.0

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many worksheets the teacher has left to grade after grading some and receiving additional ones. This involves subtracting the graded worksheets from the initial total, then adding the newly turned-in worksheets. A direct calculation should suffice.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial worksheets: 34
- Graded worksheets: 7
- Newly turned-in worksheets: 36
- Assumption: No worksheets are lost or discarded.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Subtract the graded worksheets from the initial total, then add the newly turned-in worksheets.  
Logic: This method follows the sequence of events described in the problem.

**Method 2:** Combine the initial worksheets and the newly turned-in worksheets first, then subtract the graded worksheets.  
Logic: This method groups the total worksheets before accounting for the graded ones.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Subtract the graded worksheets:  
   \( 34 - 7 = 27 \)  
   (Worksheets remaining after grading.)
2. Add the newly turned-in worksheets:  
   \( 27 + 36 = 63 \)  
   (Total worksheets left to grade.)

#### **Method 2:**
1. Combine the initial worksheets and the newly turned-in worksheets:  
   \( 34 + 36 = 70 \)  
   (Total worksheets before grading.)
2. Subtract the graded worksheets:  
   \( 70 - 7 = 63 \)  
   (Total worksheets left to grade.)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** mirrors the sequence of events described in the problem (subtract first, then add). This makes it easier to follow and avoids unnecessary grouping.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Subtract the graded worksheets:  
   \( 34 - 7 = 27 \)  
2. Add the newly turned-in worksheets:  
   \( 27 + 36 = 63 \)  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse-check: Start with the final total (63), subtract the newly turned-in worksheets (36), and verify the remaining worksheets (27). Then add the graded worksheets (7) to confirm the initial total (34).  
  \( 63 - 36 = 27 \), \( 27 + 7 = 34 \) ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The teacher has **63 worksheets** left to grade.  
The answer is **63**.

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Math homework: 6 pages
- Reading homework: 4 pages
- Problems per page: 3
- Total pages = Math pages + Reading pages = \(6 + 4 = 10\)

- Multiply the number of reading pages by problems per page to find reading problems.
- Add the two results to find the total number of problems.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1:

2. Total problems = \(10 \times 3 = 30\)

#### Method 2:

2. Reading problems = \(4 \times 3 = 12\)
3. Total problems = \(18 + 12 = 30\)

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:

2. Total problems = \(10 \times 3 = 30\)

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Amy initially had **26 music files** and **36 video files**.
- She deleted **48 files**.
- No files were added or lost in any other way.

2. If the deleted files exceed the music files, subtract the remaining deleted files from the video files.

### Step 3: Execute both methods

   \( 26 + 36 = 62 \)
2. Subtract the deleted files:  
   \( 62 - 48 = 14 \)

#### **Method 2:**

   \( 26 - 48 = -22 \) (This means all 26 music files are deleted, and 22 more files need to be deleted from the video files.)
2. Subtract the remaining 22 files from the video files:  
   \( 36 - 22 = 14 \)

### Step 4: Compare both methods

   \( 26 + 36 = 62 \)
2. Subtract the deleted files:  
   \( 62 - 48 = 14 \)

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many rides Edward can go on at the state fair. He starts with 79 tickets, spends 23 tickets at a booth, and uses the remaining tickets for rides, each of which costs 7 tickets. The problem involves subtraction to find the remaining tickets and division to calculate the number of rides.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total tickets Edward bought: 79
- Tickets spent at the booth: 23
- Tickets required per ride: 7
- Assumption: All remaining tickets are used for rides, and partial rides are not allowed (integer division).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
1. **Method 1 (Sequential Calculation):**
   - Subtract the tickets spent at the booth from the total tickets to find the remaining tickets.
   - Divide the remaining tickets by the cost per ride to determine the number of rides.

2. **Method 2 (Direct Calculation):**
   - Combine subtraction and division into a single step: \((\text{Total tickets} - \text{Tickets spent at booth}) \div \text{Tickets per ride}\).
   - Perform the operations in the correct order (parentheses first, then division).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1:
1. Subtract tickets spent at the booth:  
   \( 79 - 23 = 56 \) tickets remaining.
2. Divide remaining tickets by the cost per ride:  
   \( 56 \div 7 = 8 \) rides.

#### Method 2:
1. Combine subtraction and division:  
   \( (79 - 23) \div 7 = 56 \div 7 = 8 \) rides.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result and are equally valid.
- **Method 1** is more intuitive because it breaks the problem into smaller, sequential steps, making it easier to follow and verify.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Subtract tickets spent at the booth:  
   \( 79 - 23 = 56 \) tickets remaining.
2. Divide remaining tickets by the cost per ride:  
   \( 56 \div 7 = 8 \) rides.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify subtraction: \( 79 - 23 = 56 \) ✅
- Verify division: \( 56 \div 7 = 8 \) ✅
- Reconstruct the problem: \( 8 \times 7 = 56 \), and \( 56 + 23 = 79 \) ✅

---

The answer is **8**.

Q:  A chef needs to cook 15 potatoes. He has already cooked 8. If each potato takes 9 minutes to cook, how long will it take him to cook the rest? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The chef needs to cook 15 potatoes in total but has already cooked 8. This means he still needs to cook \( 15 - 8 = 7 \) potatoes. Since each potato takes 9 minutes to cook, the total time required to cook the remaining potatoes can be calculated by multiplying the number of uncooked potatoes by the cooking time per potato.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total potatoes needed: \( 15 \)
- Potatoes already cooked: \( 8 \)
- Remaining potatoes to cook: \( 15 - 8 = 7 \)
- Time to cook each potato: \( 9 \) minutes
- Assumption: All remaining potatoes are cooked simultaneously, and no additional delays occur.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
1. **Method 1 (Direct multiplication):** Calculate the number of uncooked potatoes, then multiply by the cooking time per potato.
2. **Method 2 (Step-by-step breakdown):** Break the problem into smaller steps: first calculate the number of uncooked potatoes, then compute the total cooking time by adding the cooking time for each potato iteratively.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### Method 1: Direct multiplication
1. Calculate the number of uncooked potatoes:  
   \( 15 - 8 = 7 \)
2. Multiply the number of uncooked potatoes by the cooking time per potato:  
   \( 7 \times 9 = 63 \) minutes.

#### Method 2: Step-by-step breakdown
1. Start with the number of uncooked potatoes: \( 7 \).
2. Add the cooking time for each potato iteratively:  
   \( 9 + 9 + 9 + 9 + 9 + 9 + 9 = 63 \) minutes.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is more efficient and direct, as it uses a single multiplication step to arrive at the result.
- **Method 2** is valid but unnecessarily repetitive, making it less practical for larger numbers.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Calculate the number of uncooked potatoes:  
   \( 15 - 8 = 7 \)
2. Multiply by the cooking time per potato:  
   \( 7 \times 9 = 63 \) minutes.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Reverse-check: Divide the total cooking time by the time per potato:  
  \( 63 \div 9 = 7 \), which matches the number of uncooked potatoes.
- Recalculate using Method 2: \( 9 + 9 + 9 + 9 + 9 + 9 + 9 = 63 \). ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The total time required to cook the remaining potatoes is **63 minutes**.  
The answer is **63**.

Q:  Zoe and her friends were recycling paper for their class. For every 8 pounds they recycled they earned one point. If Zoe recycled 25 pounds and her friends recycled 23 pounds, how many points did they earn? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining the total number of points Zoe and her friends earned by recycling paper. Each point is earned for every 8 pounds of paper recycled. The total weight recycled by Zoe and her friends must first be calculated, and then divided by 8 to find the total points. We will use two distinct methods to solve this problem and verify the result.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Zoe recycled 25 pounds.
- Her friends recycled 23 pounds.
- Total weight recycled = Zoe's contribution + her friends' contribution.
- 1 point is earned for every 8 pounds recycled.
- Points are calculated by dividing the total weight by 8, and only whole points are counted (no fractional points).

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the total weight recycled by Zoe and her friends.  
2. Divide the total weight by 8 to calculate the number of points.  
3. Discard any remainder since only whole points are earned.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the points earned separately for Zoe and her friends by dividing their individual contributions by 8.  
2. Add the points earned by Zoe and her friends to get the total points.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total weight recycled = 25 + 23 = 48 pounds.  
2. Points earned = 48 ÷ 8 = 6 (no remainder).  

#### **Method 2:**
1. Points earned by Zoe = 25 ÷ 8 = 3 (remainder 1, so only 3 points).  
2. Points earned by her friends = 23 ÷ 8 = 2 (remainder 7, so only 2 points).  
3. Total points = 3 + 2 = 5.  

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** calculates the total weight first and then divides by 8, which is simpler and avoids splitting the calculation into parts.  
- **Method 2** calculates points for each person separately, which introduces rounding errors when combining results.  
- **Preferred method:** Method 1 is more accurate and efficient because it directly calculates the total points without intermediate rounding.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
1. Total weight recycled = 25 + 23 = 48 pounds.  
2. Points earned = 48 ÷ 8 = 6.  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Reverse calculation: If 6 points were earned, the total weight recycled must be 6 × 8 = 48 pounds, which matches the given total weight. ✅  

---

The answer is **6**.
EXTRACTED:
6.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
6.0

Q:  Lana picked 36 tulips and 37 roses to make flower bouquets. If she only used 70 of the flowers though, how many extra flowers did Lana pick? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Lana picked a total number of flowers (tulips and roses) but only used 70 of them for bouquets. The problem asks us to find how many extra flowers she picked, which is the difference between the total flowers picked and the flowers used. This requires a subtraction operation after summing the total flowers picked.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Tulips picked: 36
- Roses picked: 37
- Flowers used: 70
- No flowers were discarded or lost; the unused flowers are considered "extra."

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct calculation
1. Add the number of tulips and roses to find the total flowers picked.
2. Subtract the number of flowers used from the total flowers picked to find the extra flowers.

#### Method 2: Estimate and adjust
1. Round the number of tulips and roses to approximate totals (e.g., 40 tulips and 40 roses).
2. Subtract the flowers used from the estimated total, then adjust for rounding errors.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct calculation
1. Total flowers picked = 36 + 37 = 73.
2. Extra flowers = Total flowers picked - Flowers used = 73 - 70 = 3.

#### Method 2: Estimate and adjust
1. Approximate total flowers picked: 40 tulips + 40 roses = 80.
2. Subtract flowers used: 80 - 70 = 10.
3. Adjust for rounding errors: Actual total is 73, not 80, so subtract the difference (80 - 73 = 7). Final result: 10 - 7 = 3.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is more direct and avoids unnecessary rounding and adjustments. It is simpler and less prone to error.
- **Method 2** introduces estimation, which is useful for mental math but adds complexity without improving accuracy.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total flowers picked = 36 + 37 = 73.
2. Extra flowers = Total flowers picked - Flowers used = 73 - 70 = 3.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify: Flowers used + Extra flowers = Total flowers picked → 70 + 3 = 73 ✅
- Reverse calculation: Total flowers picked - Extra flowers = Flowers used → 73 - 3 = 70 ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **3**.

3.0
GROUND_TRUTH:

### Hypothesis:
Katie baked a total number of pastries (cupcakes and cookies) for the school bake sale. After the sale, she had 8 pastries left to take home. To find out how many pastries she sold, we need to subtract the number of pastries she took home from the total number she baked.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Katie baked **7 cupcakes** and **5 cookies**.
- Total pastries baked = \( 7 + 5 = 12 \).
- Katie took **8 pastries** home after the sale.
- No pastries were lost or discarded during the sale.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct subtraction
1. Calculate the total number of pastries baked.
2. Subtract the number of pastries Katie took home from the total number baked to find the number sold.

#### Method 2: Reverse calculation
1. Start with the number of pastries Katie took home.
2. Add the number of pastries sold to reach the total number baked.
3. Solve for the number sold by subtracting the number taken home from the total baked.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct subtraction
1. Total pastries baked = \( 7 + 5 = 12 \).
2. Subtract the number of pastries Katie took home: \( 12 - 8 = 4 \).
3. Katie sold **4 pastries**.

#### Method 2: Reverse calculation
1. Total pastries baked = \( 7 + 5 = 12 \).
2. Start with the number of pastries Katie took home: \( 8 \).
3. Add the number sold to reach the total baked: \( 8 + x = 12 \).
4. Solve for \( x \): \( x = 12 - 8 = 4 \).
5. Katie sold **4 pastries**.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it involves fewer steps and avoids unnecessary reverse reasoning. Therefore, Method 1 is preferred.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total pastries baked = \( 7 + 5 = 12 \).
2. Subtract the number of pastries Katie took home: \( 12 - 8 = 4 \).
3. Katie sold **4 pastries**.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Total pastries baked = \( 7 + 5 = 12 \).
- Katie took home \( 8 \) pastries.
- Sold pastries = \( 12 - 8 = 4 \).
- Verify: \( 4 + 8 = 12 \), which matches the total baked.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **4**.

Q:  At the fair Kaleb bought 6 tickets. After riding the ferris wheel he had 3 tickets left. If each ticket cost 9 dollars, how much money did Kaleb spend riding the ferris wheel? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how much money Kaleb spent riding the ferris wheel. To do this, we need to calculate how many tickets he used for the ride and then multiply that number by the cost per ticket. The general strategy involves subtraction to find the number of tickets used and multiplication to find the total cost.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total tickets Kaleb bought: 6 tickets
- Tickets remaining after the ferris wheel: 3 tickets
- Cost per ticket: $9
- Assumption: All tickets used were for the ferris wheel, and no tickets were lost or used elsewhere.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Subtract the remaining tickets from the total tickets to find the number of tickets used.  
2. Multiply the number of tickets used by the cost per ticket to find the total cost.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the total cost of all 6 tickets Kaleb bought.  
2. Calculate the cost of the 3 tickets he has left.  
3. Subtract the cost of the remaining tickets from the total cost to find the amount spent on the ferris wheel.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

**Method 1:**  
1. Tickets used = Total tickets - Remaining tickets = 6 - 3 = 3 tickets.  
2. Total cost = Tickets used × Cost per ticket = 3 × $9 = $27.

**Method 2:**  
1. Total cost of all tickets = Total tickets × Cost per ticket = 6 × $9 = $54.  
2. Cost of remaining tickets = Remaining tickets × Cost per ticket = 3 × $9 = $27.  
3. Cost of ferris wheel = Total cost - Cost of remaining tickets = $54 - $27 = $27.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more direct and involves fewer steps. It is therefore the preferred method.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method
1. Tickets used = 6 - 3 = 3 tickets.  
2. Total cost = 3 × $9 = $27.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy
- Reverse-check: If Kaleb spent $27, then he used 3 tickets (since $27 ÷ $9 = 3). Adding the 3 tickets he has left, the total is 6 tickets, which matches the original total. ✅

---

The answer is **$27**.

27.0

### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining the number of books remaining in the bargain bin after a series of transactions: selling some books and adding more. The strategy involves tracking the initial count, subtracting the sold books, and then adding the newly added books.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial number of books: 4
- Books sold: 3
- Books added: 10
- No books are lost or removed beyond the transactions described.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:

**Method 2:** Combine the transactions into a single net change: Add the books added to the bin, subtract the books sold, and apply the result to the initial count.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation**
1. Start with the initial count: 4 books.
2. Subtract the books sold: \( 4 - 3 = 1 \).
3. Add the books added: \( 1 + 10 = 11 \).

Result: 11 books.

---

#### **Method 2: Combine transactions into a net change**
1. Calculate the net change: \( 10 - 3 = 7 \) (books added minus books sold).
2. Apply the net change to the initial count: \( 4 + 7 = 11 \).

Result: 11 books.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more efficient because it combines the transactions into a single step, reducing intermediate calculations. It is also easier to follow when dealing with larger numbers or more complex transactions.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Net change: \( 10 - 3 = 7 \).
2. Apply the net change: \( 4 + 7 = 11 \).

Result: 11 books.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Start with 4 books.
- Subtract 3 books sold: \( 4 - 3 = 1 \).
- Add 10 books: \( 1 + 10 = 11 \).

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Isabel bought 6 country albums and 2 pop albums, with each album containing 9 songs. To find the total number of songs Isabel purchased, we need to calculate the total number of albums and multiply by the number of songs per album. This involves basic multiplication and addition.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Number of country albums = 6
- Number of pop albums = 2
- Songs per album = 9
- All albums have the same number of songs (9 songs each).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
Add the number of country and pop albums first, then multiply the total number of albums by the number of songs per album.  

**Method 2:**  
Calculate the total number of songs for country albums and pop albums separately, then add the results.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total number of albums = 6 (country) + 2 (pop) = 8 albums.
2. Total number of songs = 8 albums × 9 songs/album = 72 songs.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Songs from country albums = 6 albums × 9 songs/album = 54 songs.
2. Songs from pop albums = 2 albums × 9 songs/album = 18 songs.
3. Total number of songs = 54 (country) + 18 (pop) = 72 songs.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is more efficient because it reduces the number of intermediate calculations. It directly combines the total number of albums before multiplying, which simplifies the process.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total number of albums = 6 + 2 = 8.
2. Total number of songs = 8 × 9 = 72.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify using Method 2:  
  Songs from country albums = 6 × 9 = 54.  
  Songs from pop albums = 2 × 9 = 18.  
  Total songs = 54 + 18 = 72. ✅  

- Reverse calculation:  
  72 ÷ 9 = 8 albums, which matches the total number of albums (6 + 2). ✅  

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **72**.

Q:  Frank had saved up 3 dollars. If he received another 37 dollars for his allowance, how many 8 dollar toys could he buy? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Frank has $3 saved and receives an additional $37, giving him a total amount of money. The goal is to determine how many $8 toys he can buy. This involves calculating the total money Frank has, dividing it by the cost of one toy, and finding the integer part of the result (since he cannot buy a fraction of a toy).

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Saved money: $3
- Allowance: $37
- Total money = Saved money + Allowance
- Cost of one toy: $8
- Frank can only buy whole toys (integer division).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct calculation using integer division
1. Add the saved money and allowance to find the total money.
2. Divide the total money by the cost of one toy using integer division to find the number of toys Frank can afford.

#### Method 2: Subtraction-based approach
1. Start with the total money (saved + allowance).
2. Repeatedly subtract the cost of one toy ($8) until the remaining money is less than $8.
3. Count the number of subtractions to determine how many toys Frank can buy.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct calculation using integer division
1. Total money = $3 + $37 = $40.
2. Number of toys = $40 ÷ $8 = 5 (integer division).

#### Method 2: Subtraction-based approach
1. Start with $40.
2. Subtract $8 repeatedly:
   - $40 - $8 = $32 (1 toy)
   - $32 - $8 = $24 (2 toys)
   - $24 - $8 = $16 (3 toys)
   - $16 - $8 = $8 (4 toys)
   - $8 - $8 = $0 (5 toys)
3. Count the subtractions: Frank can buy 5 toys.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient, as it uses integer division directly to compute the result in one step.
- **Method 2** is valid but involves repetitive calculations, making it less efficient for larger numbers.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total money = $3 + $37 = $40.
2. Number of toys = $40 ÷ $8 = 5.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify by multiplying: 5 toys × $8 = $40, which matches the total money Frank has.
- Remaining money after buying 5 toys: $40 - $40 = $0, confirming no leftover funds.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **5**.

5.0

Q:  Sarah baked 38 cupcakes for her school's bake sale. If her brother, Todd, ate 14 of them how many packages could she make if she put 8 cupcake in each package? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many packages of cupcakes Sarah can make after her brother Todd eats some of them. First, we calculate how many cupcakes remain after Todd eats 14. Then, we divide the remaining cupcakes by the number of cupcakes per package (8) to find the number of full packages she can make. Since partial packages are not allowed, we will use integer division.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total cupcakes baked: 38
- Cupcakes eaten by Todd: 14
- Cupcakes per package: 8
- Assumption: Only full packages are allowed (no partial packages).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Subtract the number of cupcakes Todd ate from the total to find the remaining cupcakes.  
2. Divide the remaining cupcakes by 8 and take the integer part of the result (floor division).  

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the total cupcakes Todd leaves uneaten directly.  
2. Use repeated subtraction to determine how many full packages of 8 can be made.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1: Subtraction and division**
1. Remaining cupcakes = Total cupcakes - Cupcakes eaten = \( 38 - 14 = 24 \).  
2. Number of full packages = \( \lfloor 24 \div 8 \rfloor = 3 \).

#### **Method 2: Repeated subtraction**
1. Start with 24 remaining cupcakes (as calculated above).  
2. Subtract 8 repeatedly until fewer than 8 remain:  
   - \( 24 - 8 = 16 \) (1 package)  
   - \( 16 - 8 = 8 \) (2 packages)  
   - \( 8 - 8 = 0 \) (3 packages).  
3. Count the number of subtractions: 3 full packages.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient because it uses direct division.  
- **Method 2** is valid but slower due to repeated subtraction.  

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Remaining cupcakes = \( 38 - 14 = 24 \).  
2. Number of full packages = \( \lfloor 24 \div 8 \rfloor = 3 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Verify: \( 3 \times 8 = 24 \), which matches the remaining cupcakes. ✅  
- Verify: \( 24 + 14 = 38 \), confirming the total cupcakes. ✅  

---

The answer is **3**.
EXTRACTED:
3.0
GROUND_TRUTH:

Q:  Oliver is at the library helping put away books. There are 46 book to put away total but a librarian takes 10 of them and leaves Oliver with the rest. If he can fit 4 books on a shelf, how many shelves will he need? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Oliver needs to determine how many shelves are required to store the books he is left with after the librarian takes some away. First, calculate the number of books Oliver has left, then divide this number by the capacity of each shelf (4 books per shelf). If the result is not a whole number, round up because partial shelves cannot exist.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total books: 46
- Books taken by the librarian: 10
- Books per shelf: 4
- Assumption: Oliver must use full shelves, so any remainder requires an additional shelf.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
Subtract the books taken by the librarian from the total, then divide the remaining books by the shelf capacity. Use division and round up if necessary.

**Method 2:**  
Subtract the books taken by the librarian, then repeatedly subtract the shelf capacity (4 books) until the remaining books are less than the shelf capacity. Count the number of subtractions to determine the number of shelves.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Direct division**
1. Books left after the librarian takes 10:  
   \( 46 - 10 = 36 \)
2. Divide the remaining books by the shelf capacity:  
   \( 36 \div 4 = 9 \)  
   Since the result is a whole number, no rounding is needed.

#### **Method 2: Iterative subtraction**
1. Books left after the librarian takes 10:  
   \( 46 - 10 = 36 \)
2. Subtract the shelf capacity repeatedly:  
   - \( 36 - 4 = 32 \) (1 shelf)  
   - \( 32 - 4 = 28 \) (2 shelves)  
   - \( 28 - 4 = 24 \) (3 shelves)  
   - \( 24 - 4 = 20 \) (4 shelves)  
   - \( 20 - 4 = 16 \) (5 shelves)  
   - \( 16 - 4 = 12 \) (6 shelves)  
   - \( 12 - 4 = 8 \) (7 shelves)  
   - \( 8 - 4 = 4 \) (8 shelves)  
   - \( 4 - 4 = 0 \) (9 shelves)  
   Total shelves required: 9.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient because it uses division directly, avoiding repetitive subtraction. It also handles rounding automatically if needed.
- **Method 2** is valid but unnecessarily tedious for larger numbers.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Books left after the librarian takes 10:  
   \( 46 - 10 = 36 \)
2. Divide the remaining books by the shelf capacity:  
   \( 36 \div 4 = 9 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify by multiplying the number of shelves by the shelf capacity:  
  \( 9 \times 4 = 36 \), which matches the number of books left. ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **9**.

9.0

#### **Method 2: Combine treasures first, then multiply**
1. Total treasures: \( 4 + 3 = 7 \)
2. Total score: \( 7 \times 5 = 35 \)

Q:  A painter needed to paint 12 rooms in a building. Each room takes 3 hours to paint. If he already painted 4 rooms, how much longer will he take to paint the rest? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how much longer the painter will take to paint the remaining rooms. We know the total number of rooms, the time it takes to paint each room, and how many rooms have already been painted. The strategy involves calculating the number of rooms left to paint and multiplying that by the time required per room.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total rooms to paint: 12
- Time to paint each room: 3 hours
- Rooms already painted: 4
- Remaining rooms = Total rooms - Rooms already painted
- No interruptions or changes in painting speed are assumed.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Subtract the number of rooms already painted from the total number of rooms to find the remaining rooms.  
2. Multiply the remaining rooms by the time required to paint each room.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the total time required to paint all 12 rooms.  
2. Subtract the time already spent painting the 4 completed rooms from the total time.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1: Subtract rooms, then calculate time**
1. Remaining rooms = Total rooms - Rooms already painted  
   \( 12 - 4 = 8 \) rooms remain.  
2. Time to paint remaining rooms = Remaining rooms × Time per room  
   \( 8 \times 3 = 24 \) hours.

#### **Method 2: Subtract time already spent from total time**
1. Total time to paint all rooms = Total rooms × Time per room  
   \( 12 \times 3 = 36 \) hours.  
2. Time already spent = Rooms already painted × Time per room  
   \( 4 \times 3 = 12 \) hours.  
3. Time remaining = Total time - Time already spent  
   \( 36 - 12 = 24 \) hours.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more direct because it focuses on the remaining rooms and avoids unnecessary intermediate calculations. It is simpler and less prone to error.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Remaining rooms = \( 12 - 4 = 8 \).  
2. Time to paint remaining rooms = \( 8 \times 3 = 24 \) hours.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Total time to paint all rooms = \( 12 \times 3 = 36 \) hours.  
- Time already spent = \( 4 \times 3 = 12 \) hours.  
- Time remaining = \( 36 - 12 = 24 \) hours.  
The calculations are consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The painter will take **24 hours** to paint the remaining rooms.  
The answer is **24**.

### Hypothesis:
Debby took pictures at two locations during her vacation and later deleted some of them. We need to calculate how many pictures she still has by summing the pictures taken at the zoo and museum, then subtracting the deleted pictures. This involves addition followed by subtraction.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Pictures taken at the zoo: 24
- Pictures taken at the museum: 12
- Pictures deleted: 14
- Assumption: All deleted pictures were from the total vacation pictures, and no additional pictures were deleted or added.

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Sequential calculation
1. Add the pictures taken at the zoo and museum to find the total number of pictures taken during the vacation.
2. Subtract the number of deleted pictures from the total.

#### Method 2: Combine operations
1. Subtract the deleted pictures from the sum of the zoo and museum pictures in a single step.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:
#### Method 1:
1. Add the pictures taken at the zoo and museum:  
   \( 24 + 12 = 36 \)
2. Subtract the deleted pictures:  
   \( 36 - 14 = 22 \)

#### Method 2:
1. Combine operations into a single step:  
   \( (24 + 12) - 14 = 36 - 14 = 22 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** breaks the problem into smaller steps, which is easier to follow for beginners or when working with larger numbers.
- **Method 2** is more efficient and avoids intermediate calculations, but it requires careful attention to parentheses and order of operations.
- Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is preferred for clarity and simplicity.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Add the pictures taken at the zoo and museum:  
   \( 24 + 12 = 36 \)
2. Subtract the deleted pictures:  
   \( 36 - 14 = 22 \)

---

1. Reverse the subtraction:  
   \( 22 + 14 = 36 \) (matches the total pictures taken)

   \( 24 + 12 = 36 \) (matches the total pictures taken)

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many pieces of chocolate candy Will still has after giving away 3 boxes to his little brother. First, we calculate the total number of pieces in all 7 boxes, then subtract the number of pieces in the 3 boxes Will gave away. This involves multiplication and subtraction.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total boxes Will bought: 7
- Boxes given to his brother: 3
- Pieces of candy per box: 4
- Assumption: All boxes contain the same number of pieces (4 pieces per box).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total number of pieces in all 7 boxes by multiplying the number of boxes by the number of pieces per box.  
2. Calculate the number of pieces in the 3 boxes given away.  
3. Subtract the pieces in the 3 boxes from the total pieces.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the 3 boxes given away from the total 7 boxes to find the number of boxes Will still has.  
2. Multiply the remaining number of boxes by the number of pieces per box to find the total pieces Will still has.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total pieces in 7 boxes:  
   \( 7 \times 4 = 28 \) pieces.  
2. Pieces in 3 boxes given away:  
   \( 3 \times 4 = 12 \) pieces.  
3. Remaining pieces:  
   \( 28 - 12 = 16 \) pieces.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Remaining boxes after giving away 3:  
   \( 7 - 3 = 4 \) boxes.  
2. Total pieces in the remaining 4 boxes:  
   \( 4 \times 4 = 16 \) pieces.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more efficient because it avoids calculating the total number of pieces in all 7 boxes, which is unnecessary for finding the remaining pieces. Method 2 directly focuses on the remaining boxes.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 2):
1. Remaining boxes: \( 7 - 3 = 4 \).  
2. Total pieces in remaining boxes: \( 4 \times 4 = 16 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result:
- Total pieces in 7 boxes: \( 7 \times 4 = 28 \).  
- Pieces in 3 boxes given away: \( 3 \times 4 = 12 \).  
- Remaining pieces: \( 28 - 12 = 16 \). ✅

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Short sleeve shirts: 29
- Long sleeve shirts: 11
- Shirts washed: 35
- Total shirts = short sleeve + long sleeve
- No shirts are lost or damaged.

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct calculation of total shirts, then subtract washed shirts.
1. Add the short sleeve and long sleeve shirts to find the total number of shirts.
2. Subtract the number of washed shirts from the total to find the number of unwashed shirts.

#### Method 2: Calculate unwashed shirts directly using subtraction for each type of shirt.
1. Subtract the washed shirts from the total number of short sleeve shirts and long sleeve shirts combined.
2. Verify the result by summing the washed and unwashed shirts to ensure it matches the total.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1:
1. Total shirts = 29 (short sleeve) + 11 (long sleeve) = 40 shirts.
2. Unwashed shirts = Total shirts - Washed shirts = 40 - 35 = 5 shirts.

#### Method 2:
1. Total shirts = 29 + 11 = 40 shirts.
2. Unwashed shirts = Total shirts - Washed shirts = 40 - 35 = 5 shirts.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:

Preferred method: **Method 1**

---

1. Total shirts = 29 + 11 = 40.

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many extra apples the cafeteria has after 21 students take one apple each. To solve this, we need to calculate the total number of apples ordered, subtract the number of apples taken by students, and find the remainder. A direct subtraction approach should suffice.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Red apples ordered: 33
- Green apples ordered: 23
- Total students wanting fruit: 21
- Each student takes one apple.
- No apples are lost or discarded.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total number of apples ordered by adding red and green apples.  
2. Subtract the number of apples taken by students from the total apples to find the remainder.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the number of students from the red apples first.  
2. If there are still students left, subtract the remaining students from the green apples.  
3. Add the leftover apples from both groups to find the total extra apples.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total apples = Red apples + Green apples = 33 + 23 = 56.  
2. Apples taken by students = 21.  
3. Extra apples = Total apples - Apples taken = 56 - 21 = 35.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Start with red apples: 33 - 21 = 12 apples left (all students accounted for using red apples).  
2. Green apples remain untouched: 23 apples.  
3. Total extra apples = Leftover red apples + Green apples = 12 + 23 = 35.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient, as it calculates the total apples first and subtracts the number taken by students in one step.  
- **Method 2** is valid but involves unnecessary intermediate steps, making it less efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total apples = 33 + 23 = 56.  
2. Apples taken by students = 21.  
3. Extra apples = 56 - 21 = 35.

---
