Q:  For a birthday party Jerry bought 41 regular sodas and 22 diet sodas. If his fridge would only hold 9 on each shelf, how many shelves would he fill up? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many shelves Jerry will need to store all the sodas he bought. First, we calculate the total number of sodas (regular + diet). Then, we divide this total by the capacity of each shelf (9 sodas per shelf) and round up to account for any partial shelf.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Regular sodas: 41
- Diet sodas: 22
- Fridge capacity per shelf: 9 sodas
- Assumption: All sodas are stored in the fridge, and no shelf is left partially filled unless necessary.

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the total number of sodas.  
2. Divide the total by the shelf capacity (9).  
3. Round up to the nearest whole number since partial shelves still require a full shelf.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate how many full shelves are needed for the regular sodas and diet sodas separately.  
2. Add the number of shelves required for each type of soda.  
3. Round up for any partial shelves.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### **Method 1: Total first, then divide**
1. Total sodas = 41 (regular) + 22 (diet) = 63 sodas.  
2. Divide total by shelf capacity: \( 63 \div 9 = 7 \).  
3. Since 63 is a multiple of 9, no rounding is needed. Total shelves = 7.

---

#### **Method 2: Separate calculations for regular and diet sodas**
1. Shelves for regular sodas: \( 41 \div 9 = 4.555 \). Round up to 5 shelves.  
2. Shelves for diet sodas: \( 22 \div 9 = 2.444 \). Round up to 3 shelves.  
3. Total shelves = 5 (regular) + 3 (diet) = 8 shelves.  
4. However, this method overestimates because it does not combine the sodas efficiently.

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
- **Method 1** is more efficient because it combines all sodas before dividing, ensuring no unnecessary rounding errors.  
- **Method 2** overestimates the number of shelves by treating regular and diet sodas separately, which leads to inefficiency.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total sodas = 41 + 22 = 63.  
2. Divide by shelf capacity: \( 63 \div 9 = 7 \).  
3. Total shelves needed = 7.

---

### Step 6: Double-check
1. Verify total sodas: \( 41 + 22 = 63 \).  
2. Verify shelf calculation: \( 63 \div 9 = 7 \).  
3. Verify by reverse calculation: \( 7 \times 9 = 63 \), which matches the total number of sodas.

---

The answer is **7**.
EXTRACTED:
7.0

Q:  There school's baseball team had 4 new players and 6 returning players. If the coach put them into groups with 5 players in each group, how many groups would there be? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many groups of 5 players can be formed from the total number of players on the baseball team. To solve this, we need to calculate the total number of players and then divide that number by the group size (5). The result will be the number of groups.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Number of new players: 4
- Number of returning players: 6
- Group size: 5 players per group
- Assumption: All players are grouped evenly, and no players are left out.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Add the number of new and returning players to find the total, then divide the total by the group size directly.  
**Method 2:** Divide each type of player (new and returning) by the group size separately, then add the results to find the total number of groups.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Direct addition and division**
1. Add the number of new and returning players:  
   \( 4 + 6 = 10 \) (total players).
2. Divide the total number of players by the group size:  
   \( 10 \div 5 = 2 \) (groups).

#### **Method 2: Separate division and addition**
1. Divide the number of new players by the group size:  
   \( 4 \div 5 = 0.8 \) (not a full group, so round down to 0 full groups).  
   Note: This method assumes only full groups are counted.
2. Divide the number of returning players by the group size:  
   \( 6 \div 5 = 1.2 \) (round down to 1 full group).  
3. Add the results:  
   \( 0 + 1 = 1 \) (groups).  
   **Note:** This method is flawed because it does not account for combining players from both categories.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is preferred because it correctly combines all players before dividing by the group size, ensuring no players are excluded from forming groups.  
- **Method 2** is flawed because it treats new and returning players separately, which leads to incorrect results when players can be combined into groups.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Add the number of new and returning players:  
   \( 4 + 6 = 10 \) (total players).
2. Divide the total number of players by the group size:  
   \( 10 \div 5 = 2 \) (groups).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify the total number of players: \( 4 + 6 = 10 \).  
- Verify the division: \( 10 \div 5 = 2 \).  
- Confirm that all players are accounted for and grouped evenly.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **2**.
EXTRACTED:
2.0

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many problems the teacher still needs to grade. Each worksheet contains 2 problems, and the teacher has already graded 7 out of 14 worksheets. To find the remaining problems, we need to calculate the number of ungraded worksheets and multiply that by the number of problems per worksheet.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total worksheets: 14
- Worksheets already graded: 7
- Problems per worksheet: 2
- Ungraded worksheets = Total worksheets - Graded worksheets = \( 14 - 7 \)
- No additional worksheets or problems are added or removed.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct subtraction and multiplication
1. Subtract the number of graded worksheets from the total worksheets to find the ungraded worksheets.
2. Multiply the number of ungraded worksheets by the number of problems per worksheet.

#### Method 2: Calculate total problems first, then subtract graded problems
1. Calculate the total number of problems across all worksheets.
2. Calculate the number of problems already graded.
3. Subtract the graded problems from the total problems to find the remaining problems.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1: Direct subtraction and multiplication
1. Ungraded worksheets = \( 14 - 7 = 7 \)
2. Remaining problems = \( 7 \times 2 = 14 \)

#### Method 2: Calculate total problems first, then subtract graded problems
1. Total problems = \( 14 \times 2 = 28 \)
2. Graded problems = \( 7 \times 2 = 14 \)
3. Remaining problems = \( 28 - 14 = 14 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it avoids unnecessary intermediate calculations. It focuses directly on the ungraded worksheets and their problems.
- **Method 2** is valid but involves extra steps, making it less efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Ungraded worksheets = \( 14 - 7 = 7 \)
2. Remaining problems = \( 7 \times 2 = 14 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Total problems = \( 14 \times 2 = 28 \)
- Graded problems = \( 7 \times 2 = 14 \)
- Remaining problems = \( 28 - 14 = 14 \) ✅

The teacher has 14 more problems to grade.

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many weeks Jerry's earnings will last if he spends $5 per week. First, we calculate Jerry's total earnings by adding the money he made mowing lawns and weed eating. Then, we divide this total by his weekly spending to find the number of weeks the money will last.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Money earned mowing lawns: $14
- Money earned weed eating: $31
- Weekly spending: $5
- Assumption: Jerry spends exactly $5 per week, and no additional money is earned or spent.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:**  
1. Add Jerry's earnings to find the total amount of money he has.  
2. Divide the total by his weekly spending to calculate the number of weeks the money will last.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract $5 repeatedly from Jerry's total earnings until the remaining amount is less than $5.  
2. Count the number of subtractions to determine the number of weeks.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Direct calculation using division**
1. Total earnings = $14 + $31 = $45  
2. Divide total earnings by weekly spending:  
   \( 45 \div 5 = 9 \) weeks.

#### **Method 2: Repeated subtraction**
1. Start with $45 and subtract $5 repeatedly:  
   - Week 1: $45 - $5 = $40  
   - Week 2: $40 - $5 = $35  
   - Week 3: $35 - $5 = $30  
   - Week 4: $30 - $5 = $25  
   - Week 5: $25 - $5 = $20  
   - Week 6: $20 - $5 = $15  
   - Week 7: $15 - $5 = $10  
   - Week 8: $10 - $5 = $5  
   - Week 9: $5 - $5 = $0  
2. Count the number of subtractions: 9 weeks.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient, as it uses division directly to calculate the number of weeks.  
- **Method 2** is valid but involves unnecessary repetitive steps, making it less practical for larger numbers.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using the preferred method:
1. Total earnings = $14 + $31 = $45  
2. Divide total earnings by weekly spending:  
   \( 45 \div 5 = 9 \) weeks.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify by multiplying the number of weeks by weekly spending:  
  \( 9 \times 5 = 45 \), which matches Jerry's total earnings. ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **9 weeks**.

Q:  The school cafeteria had 17 apples. If they used 2 to make lunch for the students and then bought 23 more, how many apples would they have? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine the total number of apples the cafeteria has after using some for lunch and purchasing more. This involves subtracting the apples used and then adding the apples purchased to the initial count. A direct calculation should suffice.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial apples: 17
- Apples used for lunch: 2
- Apples purchased: 23
- No apples are lost or discarded beyond the 2 used for lunch.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Sequential calculation: Subtract the apples used first, then add the apples purchased.  
**Method 2:** Combine the apples used and purchased into a single net change, then apply it to the initial count.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation**
1. Subtract the apples used:  
   \( 17 - 2 = 15 \)
2. Add the apples purchased:  
   \( 15 + 23 = 38 \)

#### **Method 2: Combine net change**
1. Combine the apples used and purchased into a single net change:  
   \( -2 + 23 = +21 \)
2. Apply the net change to the initial count:  
   \( 17 + 21 = 38 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is more intuitive because it mirrors the sequence of events described in the problem (subtract first, then add).  
- **Method 2** is slightly faster but requires combining operations, which may be less clear for some learners.  
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 1** is preferred for clarity and alignment with the problem's narrative.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Subtract the apples used:  
   \( 17 - 2 = 15 \)
2. Add the apples purchased:  
   \( 15 + 23 = 38 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse-check: Start with the final count (38), subtract the apples purchased (23), and add back the apples used (2):  
  \( 38 - 23 = 15 \), \( 15 + 2 = 17 \) ✅  
- Both methods agree, and the calculations are consistent.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **38**.
EXTRACTED:
38.0

Q:  Jerry was helping the cafeteria workers pick up lunch trays, but he could only carry 8 trays at a time. If he had to pick up 9 trays from one table and 7 trays from another, how many trips will he make? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many trips Jerry will need to make to carry all the lunch trays, given that he can carry a maximum of 8 trays per trip. The total number of trays is the sum of trays from two tables, and the number of trips is the total trays divided by the capacity per trip, rounded up to the nearest whole number since partial trips are not possible.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Jerry can carry **8 trays per trip**.
- Trays from the first table: **9 trays**.
- Trays from the second table: **7 trays**.
- Total trays = 9 + 7 = **16 trays**.
- Jerry must make whole trips, so any remainder requires an additional trip.

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods
**Method 1: Direct division and rounding up**
- Divide the total number of trays by the number of trays Jerry can carry per trip.
- If the result is not a whole number, round up to the nearest integer.

**Method 2: Incremental counting**
- Start with the total number of trays and subtract 8 trays (one trip) repeatedly until no trays remain.
- Count the number of subtractions (trips).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods

#### Method 1: Direct division and rounding up
1. Total trays = 16.
2. Trays per trip = 8.
3. Divide: \( 16 \div 8 = 2 \) (exactly 2 trips, no remainder).
4. Since the result is a whole number, no rounding is needed.

**Result from Method 1: 2 trips.**

---

#### Method 2: Incremental counting
1. Start with 16 trays.
2. Subtract 8 trays for the first trip: \( 16 - 8 = 8 \).
3. Subtract 8 trays for the second trip: \( 8 - 8 = 0 \).
4. No trays remain after 2 trips.

**Result from Method 2: 2 trips.**

---

### Step 4: Compare methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is more efficient because it uses a single division and rounding step, while Method 2 involves multiple subtractions. Therefore, Method 1 is preferred.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method
1. Total trays = 16.
2. Trays per trip = 8.
3. Divide: \( 16 \div 8 = 2 \).
4. Since the result is a whole number, Jerry needs exactly 2 trips.

---

### Step 6: Double-check
- Total trays = 16.
- Jerry carries 8 trays per trip.
- In 2 trips, Jerry can carry \( 8 \times 2 = 16 \) trays, which matches the total.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **2**.
EXTRACTED:
2.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
2.0

Q:  A florist had 5 roses. If she sold 3 of them and then later picked 34 more, how many roses would she have? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many roses the florist has after selling some and then picking more. This involves subtracting the number of roses sold from the initial amount and then adding the newly picked roses. A step-by-step calculation will ensure accuracy.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Initial number of roses: 5
- Roses sold: 3
- Roses picked later: 34
- Assumption: No roses are lost or discarded during the process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:** Sequential calculation  
1. Subtract the roses sold from the initial amount.  
2. Add the newly picked roses to the remaining roses.

**Method 2:** Combine operations into a single equation  
1. Start with the initial number of roses.  
2. Subtract the roses sold and add the newly picked roses in one step.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### Method 1: Sequential calculation
1. Start with 5 roses.  
2. Subtract the 3 roses sold:  
   \( 5 - 3 = 2 \) roses remain.  
3. Add the 34 roses picked:  
   \( 2 + 34 = 36 \).  

#### Method 2: Combined operation
1. Combine subtraction and addition into one equation:  
   \( 5 - 3 + 34 = 36 \).  

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is more detailed and breaks the problem into smaller steps, which can help avoid errors.  
- **Method 2** is more efficient and concise but requires careful attention to the order of operations.  
Both methods yield the same result, but Method 1 is preferred for clarity in this case.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Start with 5 roses.  
2. Subtract the 3 roses sold:  
   \( 5 - 3 = 2 \).  
3. Add the 34 roses picked:  
   \( 2 + 34 = 36 \).  

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result

  \( 36 - 34 = 2 \), \( 2 + 3 = 5 \) (matches the initial count). ✅

Q:  For homework Faye had 13 math problems and 2 spelling problems. If she can finish 3 problems in an hour how long will it take her to finish all the problems? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how long it will take Faye to complete all her homework problems if she can finish 3 problems per hour. This involves calculating the total number of problems and dividing that by her rate of completion. The result will give us the total time required.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Faye has **13 math problems** and **2 spelling problems**.
- Total problems = \( 13 + 2 = 15 \).
- Faye can complete **3 problems per hour**.
- We assume Faye works at a constant rate and does not take breaks.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
#### Method 1: Direct division
- Divide the total number of problems by the rate of completion (problems per hour) to find the time required.

#### Method 2: Incremental counting
- Start with the total number of problems and subtract 3 problems per hour until all problems are completed. Count the number of hours required.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods
#### Method 1: Direct division
1. Total problems = \( 15 \).
2. Rate of completion = \( 3 \) problems per hour.
3. Time required = \( \frac{15}{3} = 5 \) hours.

#### Method 2: Incremental counting
1. Start with \( 15 \) problems.
2. Subtract \( 3 \) problems per hour:
   - After 1 hour: \( 15 - 3 = 12 \) problems remain.
   - After 2 hours: \( 12 - 3 = 9 \) problems remain.
   - After 3 hours: \( 9 - 3 = 6 \) problems remain.
   - After 4 hours: \( 6 - 3 = 3 \) problems remain.
   - After 5 hours: \( 3 - 3 = 0 \) problems remain.
3. Total time = \( 5 \) hours.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient because it uses a single division operation.
- **Method 2** is valid but involves repetitive calculations, making it less efficient.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total problems = \( 15 \).
2. Rate of completion = \( 3 \) problems per hour.
3. Time required = \( \frac{15}{3} = 5 \) hours.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- If Faye works for \( 5 \) hours at \( 3 \) problems per hour, she completes \( 5 \times 3 = 15 \) problems, which matches the total number of problems. ✅

---

The answer is **5 hours**.

Q:  Faye and her mom were picking carrots from their garden. Faye picked 23 and her mother picked 5. If only 12 of the carrots were good, how many bad carrots did they have? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with finding the number of bad carrots. Faye and her mom picked a total number of carrots, and we know how many of them were good. The bad carrots are the difference between the total carrots picked and the good carrots. This requires summing the carrots picked by Faye and her mom, then subtracting the number of good carrots.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Faye picked 23 carrots.
- Faye's mom picked 5 carrots.
- Total good carrots = 12.
- All carrots picked are either good or bad (no other categories).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
#### Method 1: Direct subtraction after summing the total carrots.
1. Compute the total number of carrots picked by summing Faye's and her mom's contributions.
2. Subtract the number of good carrots from the total to find the bad carrots.

#### Method 2: Separate contributions of bad carrots from each person.
1. Subtract the good carrots from the total carrots picked by Faye and her mom individually.
2. Sum the bad carrots from both contributions.

---

### Step 3: Carry out both methods step-by-step:

#### Method 1:
1. Total carrots picked = Faye's carrots + Mom's carrots = 23 + 5 = 28.
2. Bad carrots = Total carrots - Good carrots = 28 - 12 = 16.

#### Method 2:
1. Faye's bad carrots = Faye's total carrots - her share of good carrots. Assume Faye's share of good carrots is proportional to her total contribution:
   - Faye's share of good carrots = (23 / 28) × 12 ≈ 9.86 (round to 10).
   - Faye's bad carrots = 23 - 10 = 13.
2. Mom's bad carrots = Mom's total carrots - her share of good carrots:
   - Mom's share of good carrots = (5 / 28) × 12 ≈ 2.14 (round to 2).
   - Mom's bad carrots = 5 - 2 = 3.
3. Total bad carrots = Faye's bad carrots + Mom's bad carrots = 13 + 3 = 16.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is simpler and avoids unnecessary proportional calculations. It directly computes the total carrots and subtracts the good ones, which is sufficient for this problem.
- **Method 2** introduces proportionality, which is unnecessary since the problem does not specify that good carrots are divided between Faye and her mom.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Total carrots picked = 23 + 5 = 28.
2. Bad carrots = Total carrots - Good carrots = 28 - 12 = 16.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse check: Total carrots = Good carrots + Bad carrots = 12 + 16 = 28 ✅
- Both methods yield the same result, confirming accuracy.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **16**.
EXTRACTED:
16.0
GROUND_TRUTH:
16.0

Q:  In a video game, each enemy defeated gives you 5 points. If a level has 8 enemies total and you destroy all but 6 of them, how many points would you earn? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to calculate the total points earned in a video game by defeating some enemies. Each enemy defeated gives 5 points, and the level has 8 enemies in total. If 6 enemies remain undefeated, we need to determine how many enemies were defeated and then calculate the total points earned. The strategy involves finding the number of defeated enemies and multiplying it by the points per enemy.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Total enemies in the level: 8
- Enemies left undefeated: 6
- Points per enemy defeated: 5
- Assumption: All defeated enemies give the same number of points, and no partial points are awarded.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
Subtract the number of undefeated enemies from the total number of enemies to find the number of defeated enemies. Then multiply the defeated enemies by the points per enemy.

**Method 2:**  
Directly calculate the number of defeated enemies by multiplying the points per enemy by the total number of enemies and subtracting the points corresponding to the undefeated enemies.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### Method 1:
1. Calculate the number of defeated enemies:  
   \( 8 - 6 = 2 \)
2. Multiply the defeated enemies by the points per enemy:  
   \( 2 \times 5 = 10 \)

#### Method 2:
1. Calculate the total possible points if all enemies were defeated:  
   \( 8 \times 5 = 40 \)
2. Subtract the points corresponding to the undefeated enemies:  
   \( 40 - (6 \times 5) = 40 - 30 = 10 \)

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 1** is simpler and more intuitive because it directly calculates the number of defeated enemies and then computes the points. **Method 2** involves an extra step of calculating the total possible points, which is unnecessary for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method
1. Calculate the number of defeated enemies:  
   \( 8 - 6 = 2 \)
2. Multiply the defeated enemies by the points per enemy:  
   \( 2 \times 5 = 10 \)

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy
- If 2 enemies were defeated, the points earned should be \( 2 \times 5 = 10 \).  
- If 6 enemies were undefeated, the points lost should be \( 6 \times 5 = 30 \), leaving \( 40 - 30 = 10 \).  
Both checks confirm the result.

---

The answer is **10**.

10.0

Q:  Paul had saved up 3 dollars. If he received another 7 dollars for his allowance, how many 5 dollar toys could he buy? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
Paul starts with $3 and receives an additional $7, giving him a total of $10. He wants to buy toys that cost $5 each. To determine how many toys he can buy, we need to divide his total money by the cost of one toy and ensure the result is a whole number since he cannot buy partial toys.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial savings: $3
- Allowance received: $7
- Total money: $3 + $7 = $10
- Cost of one toy: $5
- Assumption: Paul spends all his money on toys, and no partial toys can be purchased.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Direct division of total money by the cost of one toy. Use integer division to find the number of toys Paul can afford.

**Method 2:** Subtract the cost of one toy repeatedly from the total money until the remaining money is less than the cost of one toy. Count the number of subtractions.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Direct division**
1. Total money = $10
2. Cost of one toy = $5
3. Perform division: \( 10 \div 5 = 2 \)
4. Paul can afford 2 toys.

---

#### **Method 2: Repeated subtraction**
1. Start with $10.
2. Subtract $5 (cost of one toy): \( 10 - 5 = 5 \)
3. Subtract $5 again: \( 5 - 5 = 0 \)
4. Count the number of subtractions: 2 subtractions → Paul can afford 2 toys.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient, as it directly calculates the number of toys using division. It avoids unnecessary steps and is ideal for problems involving fixed costs and total amounts.
- **Method 2** is valid but less efficient, as it requires iterative calculations and counting.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
Using **Method 1**:
- Total money = $10
- Cost of one toy = $5
- Perform division: \( 10 \div 5 = 2 \)
- Paul can afford 2 toys.

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Verify: \( 2 \times 5 = 10 \), which matches the total money Paul has.
- Remaining money after buying 2 toys: \( 10 - (2 \times 5) = 0 \), confirming he spends all his money.

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **2**.

Q:  For the school bake sale Chloe made 28 cupcakes. If she sold 25 of them and then made 8 more, how many cupcakes would she have? 
RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
We are tasked with determining how many cupcakes Chloe has after selling some and then baking more. This involves subtracting the number sold from the initial amount, then adding the newly baked cupcakes. A step-by-step approach will ensure accuracy.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Chloe initially made **28 cupcakes**.
- She sold **25 cupcakes**.
- She then baked **8 more cupcakes**.
- No cupcakes were lost or discarded during the process.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Sequential operations:
1. Subtract the number of cupcakes sold from the initial amount.
2. Add the newly baked cupcakes to the remaining amount.

**Method 2:** Combine operations:
1. Calculate the net change in cupcakes (subtract sold, then add baked) in one step.
2. Apply this net change to the initial amount.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### **Method 1: Sequential operations**
1. Start with the initial number of cupcakes: **28**.
2. Subtract the number sold:  
   \( 28 - 25 = 3 \) (remaining cupcakes after selling).
3. Add the newly baked cupcakes:  
   \( 3 + 8 = 11 \).

#### **Method 2: Combine operations**
1. Calculate the net change in cupcakes:  
   \( -25 + 8 = -17 \) (net change in cupcakes due to selling and baking).
2. Apply the net change to the initial amount:  
   \( 28 + (-17) = 11 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- Both methods yield the same result (**11 cupcakes**).
- **Method 1** is more intuitive and easier to follow, as it mirrors the sequence of events (selling first, then baking).  
- **Method 2** is efficient but requires combining operations, which might be less intuitive for some.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Start with **28 cupcakes**.
2. Subtract the **25 sold**:  
   \( 28 - 25 = 3 \).
3. Add the **8 newly baked**:  
   \( 3 + 8 = 11 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result:
- Reverse the process: Start with the final count (**11 cupcakes**), subtract the newly baked (**8**), and add the sold (**25**):  
  \( 11 - 8 = 3 \), \( 3 + 25 = 28 \). ✅

---

### Final Answer:
The answer is **11**.

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many more problems the teacher has to grade. To do this, we need to calculate the total number of problems on all worksheets, subtract the number of problems already graded, and find the remaining problems. The strategy involves multiplying the number of problems per worksheet by the number of ungraded worksheets.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Each worksheet has **3 problems**.
- The teacher has **15 worksheets** in total.
- The teacher has already graded **7 worksheets**.
- The remaining worksheets to be graded are \( 15 - 7 = 8 \).

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Calculate the total number of problems on all worksheets: \( 15 \times 3 \).  
2. Calculate the number of problems already graded: \( 7 \times 3 \).  
3. Subtract the graded problems from the total problems to find the remaining problems.

**Method 2:**  
1. Calculate the number of ungraded worksheets: \( 15 - 7 \).  
2. Multiply the number of ungraded worksheets by the number of problems per worksheet: \( 8 \times 3 \).

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

**Method 1:**  
1. Total problems on all worksheets:  
   \( 15 \times 3 = 45 \).  
2. Problems already graded:  
   \( 7 \times 3 = 21 \).  
3. Remaining problems:  
   \( 45 - 21 = 24 \).

**Method 2:**  
1. Ungraded worksheets:  
   \( 15 - 7 = 8 \).  
2. Remaining problems:  
   \( 8 \times 3 = 24 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
Both methods yield the same result. However, **Method 2** is more efficient because it directly calculates the remaining problems without first finding the total number of problems. This reduces the number of intermediate steps.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 2)
1. Ungraded worksheets:  
   \( 15 - 7 = 8 \).  
2. Remaining problems:  
   \( 8 \times 3 = 24 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result
- Total problems on all worksheets: \( 15 \times 3 = 45 \).  
- Problems already graded: \( 7 \times 3 = 21 \).  
- Remaining problems: \( 45 - 21 = 24 \). ✅

This involves calculating the total number of people, dividing by the van capacity, and rounding up to the nearest whole number since partial vans are not possible.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Number of students = 22
- Number of adults = 2
- Total number of people = 22 + 2 = 24
- Van capacity = 8 people per van
- Assumption: All vans must be fully loaded except the last one, and no one is left behind.

2. **Method 2 (Incremental Packing):** Start with the total number of people and subtract the van capacity repeatedly until no more full vans can be filled. Count the number of vans used.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### Method 1: Direct Division and Ceiling
1. Total number of people = 24
2. Van capacity = 8
3. Divide: \( 24 \div 8 = 3 \) (exactly 3 vans are needed, no rounding required).

#### Method 2: Incremental Packing
1. Start with 24 people.
2. Subtract 8 (1 van filled): \( 24 - 8 = 16 \).
3. Subtract 8 (2nd van filled): \( 16 - 8 = 8 \).
4. Subtract 8 (3rd van filled): \( 8 - 8 = 0 \).
5. Total vans used = 3.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method:

2. Van capacity = 8
3. Divide: \( 24 \div 8 = 3 \).
4. Since the result is a whole number, no rounding is needed.

---

RESPONSE:
### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine how many extra apples the cafeteria has after 21 students take one apple each. To solve this, we first calculate the total number of apples ordered, then subtract the number of apples taken by the students. The difference will represent the extra apples.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions
- Red apples: 33
- Green apples: 23
- Total students who want fruit: 21
- Each student takes exactly one apple.
- No apples are lost or spoiled.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods
**Method 1:**  
1. Add the total number of apples (red + green).  
2. Subtract the number of students (21) from the total apples to find the extra apples.

**Method 2:**  
1. Subtract the number of students (21) from the red apples first.  
2. If there are still students left, subtract the remaining students from the green apples.  
3. Add any leftover apples from both groups to find the extra apples.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step

#### **Method 1:**
1. Total apples = 33 (red) + 23 (green) = 56 apples.  
2. Extra apples = Total apples - Students = 56 - 21 = 35 apples.

#### **Method 2:**
1. Start with 33 red apples. Subtract 21 students:  
   \( 33 - 21 = 12 \) red apples left.  
2. Since all students have been accounted for, the green apples (23) remain untouched.  
3. Total extra apples = 12 (red) + 23 (green) = 35 apples.

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods
- **Method 1** is more direct and efficient because it calculates the total apples first and subtracts the students in one step.  
- **Method 2** is valid but unnecessarily splits the calculation into multiple steps, which is less efficient for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1)
1. Total apples = 33 + 23 = 56.  
2. Extra apples = 56 - 21 = 35.

### Step 6: Double-check the result

- Total apples = 56. Apples left = 56 - 21 = 35.

#### Method 2: Incremental grouping

1. Total team members = 32
2. Group size = 4
3. Perform division: \( 32 \div 4 = 8 \)

#### Method 2: Incremental grouping
1. Start with 32 team members.

   - \( 32 - 4 = 28 \) (1 group)
   - \( 28 - 4 = 24 \) (2 groups)
   - \( 24 - 4 = 20 \) (3 groups)
   - \( 20 - 4 = 16 \) (4 groups)

   - \( 12 - 4 = 8 \) (6 groups)
   - \( 8 - 4 = 4 \) (7 groups)
   - \( 4 - 4 = 0 \) (8 groups)
3. Count the number of subtractions: 8 groups.

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
- **Method 1** is faster and more efficient, as it directly calculates the number of groups using division.
- **Method 2** is valid but unnecessarily lengthy for this problem.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:

3. Perform division: \( 32 \div 4 = 8 \)

### Hypothesis:
The problem asks us to determine the total number of apples the cafeteria has after using some for lunch and buying more. This involves subtracting the apples used from the initial count and then adding the newly purchased apples. A direct calculation should suffice.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Initial apples: 14
- Apples used for lunch: 13
- Apples purchased: 49
- No apples are lost or wasted beyond the 13 used for lunch.

---

### Step 2: Propose two distinct solution methods:
**Method 1:** Sequential calculation: Subtract the apples used from the initial count, then add the purchased apples.  
**Method 2:** Combine the apples used and purchased into a single net change, then apply it to the initial count.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods step-by-step:

#### **Method 1: Sequential calculation**
1. Subtract the apples used:  
   \( 14 - 13 = 1 \) (remaining apples after lunch).
2. Add the purchased apples:  
   \( 1 + 49 = 50 \).

#### **Method 2: Combine net change**
1. Combine the apples used and purchased into a single net change:  
   \( -13 + 49 = +36 \) (net change in apples).
2. Apply the net change to the initial count:  
   \( 14 + 36 = 50 \).

---

### Step 4: Compare both methods and justify the preferred one:
Both methods yield the same result, but **Method 2** is more efficient because it reduces the number of steps by combining the changes into a single operation. This makes it easier to follow and minimizes potential errors.

---

### Step 5: Solve the problem again using only the preferred method:
1. Combine the apples used and purchased:  
   \( -13 + 49 = +36 \).
2. Apply the net change to the initial count:  
   \( 14 + 36 = 50 \).

---

### Step 6: Double-check the result for consistency and accuracy:
- Reverse-check: Start with the final count (50), subtract the purchased apples (49), and add back the apples used (13):  
  \( 50 - 49 = 1 \), \( 1 + 13 = 14 \) (matches the initial count). ✅

We are tasked with determining how many pies can be made with the remaining apples after 27 are handed out. Each pie requires 4 apples. The strategy involves first calculating the number of apples left after handing out 27, then dividing the remaining apples by 4 to find the number of pies that can be made.

---

### Step 1: Known quantities and assumptions:
- Total apples: 47
- Apples handed out: 27
- Apples per pie: 4
- Assumptions: No apples are wasted, and only whole pies can be made (no fractional pies).

---

### Step 2: Two solution methods:
1. **Method 1 (Sequential subtraction and division):**
   - Subtract the apples handed out from the total to find the remaining apples.
   - Divide the remaining apples by 4 to determine the number of pies.

2. **Method 2 (Direct calculation using modular arithmetic):**
   - Subtract the apples handed out from the total to find the remaining apples.
   - Use integer division to directly compute the number of pies, ignoring any leftover apples.

---

### Step 3: Execute both methods:

#### Method 1:
1. Subtract apples handed out:  
   \( 47 - 27 = 20 \) apples remain.
2. Divide remaining apples by 4:  
   \( 20 \div 4 = 5 \) pies.

#### Method 2:
1. Subtract apples handed out:  
   \( 47 - 27 = 20 \) apples remain.
2. Use integer division to compute pies:  
   \( 20 \div 4 = 5 \) pies.

---

### Step 4: Compare methods:
Both methods yield the same result. Method 1 explicitly shows the division process, while Method 2 is more concise. Both are equally valid, but Method 1 provides more clarity for intermediate steps.

---

### Step 5: Solve using the preferred method (Method 1):
1. Subtract apples handed out:  
   \( 47 - 27 = 20 \) apples remain.
2. Divide remaining apples by 4:  
   \( 20 \div 4 = 5 \) pies.

---

### Step 6: Double-check:
1. Verify subtraction: \( 47 - 27 = 20 \) ✅  
2. Verify division: \( 20 \div 4 = 5 \) ✅
